# Paris

**Paris**[^cite_note-8] is the [capital](181337.md) and [largest city](76050805.md) of [France](5843419.md), with an estimated city population of 2.04 million in an area of 105.4 km<sup>2</sup> (40.7 sq mi), as of January 2026,[^cite_note-Pop.Paris-5] and a metropolitan population of 13.3 million (2023). Located on the [Seine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seine) in the centre of the [Île-de-France](1716427.md) region, it is the [largest metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_metropolitan_areas_in_Europe) and [fourth-most populous city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_in_the_European_Union_by_population_within_city_limits) in the [European Union](9317.md) (EU). Paris is nicknamed the "City of Light" mainly due to its leading role in the [Age of Enlightenment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_of_Enlightenment).

Administratively, Paris is divided into [twenty arrondissements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arrondissements_of_Paris) (districts), each with their own cultural identity. [Haussmann's renovation of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haussmann's_renovation_of_Paris), which created new boulevards, parks, and public works, gave birth to a modern city known as the "[capital of the 19th century](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris,_Capital_of_the_19th_Century)". Paris is a major railway, motorway, and air-transport hub; in 2024 [Charles de Gaulle Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_de_Gaulle_Airport) was the EU's [busiest airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_the_busiest_airports_in_Europe). Paris has one of the most [sustainable transportation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sustainable_transportation) systems in the world and is one of only two cities that have received the [Sustainable Transport Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sustainable_Transport_Award) twice. Its [Art Nouveau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_Nouveau)-decorated [Métro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_M%C3%A9tro) has become a symbol of the city. Paris is known for its museums and architectural landmarks: the [Louvre](17546.md) is the largest and most-visited museum in the world; the [Musée d'Orsay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mus%C3%A9e_d'Orsay), [Musée Marmottan Monet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mus%C3%A9e_Marmottan_Monet), and [Musée de l'Orangerie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mus%C3%A9e_de_l'Orangerie) are noted for their collections of French [Impressionist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impressionism) art, while the [Musée National d'Art Moderne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mus%C3%A9e_National_d'Art_Moderne), [Musée Rodin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mus%C3%A9e_Rodin), and [Musée Picasso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mus%C3%A9e_Picasso) are noted for their collections of [modern](163543.md) and [contemporary art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contemporary_art). Parts of the city along the Seine have been designated a [UNESCO](21786641.md) [World Heritage Site](44940.md) since 1991.

The [president of France](24899.md) and both houses of the [French Parliament](1009776.md) sit in Paris. Paris is home to several [United Nations](31769.md) organisations, including UNESCO, as well as other international organisations such as the [OECD](33853117.md), the [International Bureau of Weights and Measures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Bureau_of_Weights_and_Measures) (in neighbouring [Saint-Cloud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint-Cloud)), the [International Energy Agency](285292.md), the [Organisation internationale de la Francophonie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organisation_internationale_de_la_Francophonie), the [International Federation for Human Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Federation_for_Human_Rights), and the [Fédération internationale de l'Automobile](248019.md), along with European bodies such as the [European Space Agency](10363.md), the [European Banking Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Banking_Authority), and the [European Securities and Markets Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Securities_and_Markets_Authority). The city [hosts many sporting events](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sport_in_Paris), such as the [French Open](147724.md), and is the home of the [association football](10568.md) club [Paris Saint-Germain](357488.md) and the [rugby union](25405.md) club [Stade Français](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stade_Fran%C3%A7ais). Paris has also hosted the [Summer Olympics](27566.md) three times, in [1900](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1900_Summer_Olympics), [1924](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1924_Summer_Olympics) and [2024](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Summer_Olympics).

## Etymology

The ancient [oppidum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oppidum) that corresponds to the modern city of Paris was first mentioned in the mid-1st century BC by [Julius Caesar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julius_Caesar) as *Lutetia Parisiorum* ('[Lutetia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lutetia) of the [Parisii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parisii_\(France\))') and is later attested as *Parision* in the 5th century AD, then as *Paris* in 1265.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENègre1990155-9][^cite_note-Falileyev-10] During the Roman period, it was commonly known as *Lutetia* in Latin, which is interpreted as either stemming from the [Celtic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celtic_languages) root *\*lukot-* ('mouse'), or from \**luto-* ('marsh, swamp').[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELambert199438-11][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDelamarre2003211-12][^cite_note-Falileyev-10]

The name *Paris* is derived from its early inhabitants, the [Parisii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parisii_\(France\)), a [Gallic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gauls) tribe from the [Iron Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_T%C3%A8ne_culture) and the [Roman period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_period).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDelamarre2003247-13] The meaning of the Gaulish [ethnonym](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnonym) remains debated. According to [Xavier Delamarre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xavier_Delamarre), it may derive from the Celtic root *pario-* ('cauldron').[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDelamarre2003247-13] [Alfred Holder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfred_Holder) interpreted the name as 'the makers' or 'the commanders', by comparing it to the [Welsh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welsh_language) *peryff* ('lord, commander'), both possibly descending from a [Proto-Celtic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proto-Celtic) form reconstructed as \**kwar-is-io*-.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBusse2006199-14] Alternatively, [Pierre-Yves Lambert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pierre-Yves_Lambert) proposed to translate *Parisii* as the 'spear people', by connecting the first element to the [Old Irish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Irish) *carr* ('spear'), derived from an earlier \**kwar-sā*.[^cite_note-Falileyev-10]

Residents of the city are known in English as Parisians and in French as *Parisiens* ([\[paʁizjɛ̃\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/French) [//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/c/cb/Parisien2.ogg/Parisien2.ogg.mp3](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/c/cb/Parisien2.ogg/Parisien2.ogg.mp3)<sup>ⓘ</sup>). They are also pejoratively called *Parigots* ([\[paʁiɡo\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/French) [//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/b/b9/Parigot.ogg/Parigot.ogg.mp3](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/b/b9/Parigot.ogg/Parigot.ogg.mp3)<sup>ⓘ</sup>).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDottin2018535-15]

## History

Main article: [History of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Paris)

For a chronological guide, see [Timeline of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_Paris).

### Origins

Main article: [Lutetia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lutetia)



Gold coins minted by the [Parisii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parisii_\(Gaul\)), 1st century BC

The *[Parisii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parisii_\(Gaul\))* people inhabited the Paris area from around the middle of the 3rd century BC.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECunliffe2004201-16] One of the area's major north–south trade routes crossed the [Seine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seine) on the [Île de la Cité](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%8Ele_de_la_Cit%C3%A9), which gradually became an important trading centre.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELawrenceGondrand201025-17] The Parisii traded with many river towns, some as far away as the Iberian Peninsula, and minted their own coins.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchmidt200965–70-18]

[Julius Caesar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julius_Caesar) [conquered](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallic_Wars) the [Paris Basin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_Basin) for the [Roman Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Republic) in 52 BC and began the Roman settlement on Paris's [Left Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rive_Gauche).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchmidt200988–104-19] The Roman town was originally called [Lutetia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lutetia), more fully, *Lutetia Parisiorum*, "Lutetia of the Parisii", modern French *Lutèce*. It became a prosperous city with a forum, baths, temples, theatres, and an [amphitheatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amphitheatre).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchmidt2009154–167-20]

By the end of the [Western Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Roman_Empire), the town was known as *Parisius*, a [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_language) name that later became *Paris* in French.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMeunier201412-21] [Christianity](5211.md) was introduced in the middle of the 3rd century AD by Saint [Denis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Denis_of_Paris), the first Bishop of Paris: according to legend, when he refused to renounce his faith before the Roman occupiers, he was beheaded on the hill which became known as *Mons Martyrum* (Latin "Hill of Martyrs"), later "[Montmartre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montmartre)", from where he walked headless to the north of the city; the place where he fell and was buried became an important religious shrine, the [Basilica of Saint-Denis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basilica_of_Saint-Denis), and many French kings are buried there.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchmidt2009210–211-22]



The [Wall of Philippe Auguste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wall_of_Philip_II_Augustus), oldest city wall in Paris

[Clovis the Frank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clovis_I), the first king of the [Merovingian dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merovingian_dynasty), made the city his capital from 508.[^cite_note-23] As the Frankish domination of Gaul began, there was a gradual immigration by the [Franks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franks) to Paris, and the Parisian [Francien](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francien_language) dialects were born. Fortification of the [Île de la Cité](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%8Ele_de_la_Cit%C3%A9) failed to avert [sacking by Vikings in 845](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Paris_\(845\)). Still, Paris's strategic importance—with its bridges preventing ships from passing—was established by successful defence in the [Siege of Paris (885–886)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Paris_\(885%E2%80%93886\)), for which the then [Count of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Count_of_Paris) (*comte de Paris*), [Odo of France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Odo_of_France), was elected king of [West Francia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Francia).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJones199448-24] From the [Capetian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Capet) dynasty that began with the 987 election of [Hugh Capet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hugh_Capet), [Count of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Count_of_Paris) and [Duke of the Franks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duke_of_the_Franks) (*duc des Francs*), as king of a unified [West Francia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Francia), Paris gradually became the largest and most prosperous city in France.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchmidt2009210–211-22]

### High and Late Middle Ages to Louis XIV

See also: [Paris in the Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_in_the_Middle_Ages), [Paris in the 16th century](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_in_the_16th_century), and [Paris in the 17th century](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_in_the_17th_century)

The Palais de la Cité and Sainte-Chapelle, viewed from the Left Bank, from the Très Riches Heures du duc de Berry (month of June) (1410)

The [Palais de la Cité](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palais_de_la_Cit%C3%A9) and [Sainte-Chapelle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sainte-Chapelle), viewed from the Left Bank, from the [Très Riches Heures du duc de Berry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tr%C3%A8s_Riches_Heures_du_duc_de_Berry), June 1410

By the end of the 12th century, Paris had become the political, economic, religious, and cultural capital of France.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELawrenceGondrand201027-25] [Maurice de Sully](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maurice_de_Sully), bishop of Paris, started the construction of the [Notre Dame Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Notre_Dame_de_Paris) in 1163, and was completed after 182 years.[^cite_note-26] After the marshland between the river Seine and its slower 'dead arm' to its north was filled in from around the 10th century,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBussmann198522-27] Paris's cultural centre began to move to the Right Bank. In 1137, a new city marketplace (today's [Les Halles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Les_Halles)) replaced the two smaller ones on the Île de la Cité and [Place de Grève (Place de l'Hôtel de Ville)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Place_de_l'H%C3%B4tel-de-Ville_%E2%80%93_Esplanade_de_la_Lib%C3%A9ration).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEde_VitriacoHinnebusch1972262-28] The latter location housed the headquarters of Paris's river trade corporation. This organisation later became, unofficially (although formally in later years), Paris's first municipal government.

In the late 12th century, [Philip Augustus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philip_II_of_France) extended the [Louvre](17546.md) fortress to defend the city against river invasions from the west, gave the city its first walls between 1190 and 1215, rebuilt its bridges to either side of its central island, and paved its main thoroughfares.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESarmant201236–40-29] In 1190, he transformed Paris's former cathedral school into a student-teacher corporation that became the [University of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Paris) and drew students from all of Europe.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESarmant201228–29-30][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELawrenceGondrand201027-25]

With 200,000 inhabitants in 1328, Paris, then already the capital of France, was the most populous city of Europe. By comparison, London in 1300 had 80,000 inhabitants.[^cite_note-ParisDigest-31] Around 1340 the city had between 250,000 and 300,000 inhabitants.[^cite_note-32] By the early fourteenth century, so much filth had collected inside urban Europe that French and Italian cities were naming streets after human waste. In medieval Paris, several street names were inspired by *merde*, the French word for "shit".[^cite_note-33]



The [Hôtel de Sens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H%C3%B4tel_de_Sens) (c. 15th–16th), former residence of the Archbishop of Sens

During the [Hundred Years' War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hundred_Years'_War), Paris was occupied by England-friendly [Burgundian forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burgundian_State) from [1418](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_coup_d'%C3%A9tat_and_massacres_of_1418), before being occupied outright by the English when [Henry V of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_V_of_England) entered the French capital in 1420;[^cite_note-34] despite a 1429 effort by [Joan of Arc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joan_of_Arc) to liberate the city,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFierro199652–53-35] it would remain under English occupation until 1436.

In the late 16th century [French Wars of Religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Wars_of_Religion), Paris was a stronghold of the [Catholic League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_League_\(French\)), the organisers of 24 August 1572 [St. Bartholomew's Day massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Bartholomew's_Day_massacre) in which thousands of French Protestants were killed.[^cite_note-36][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBayrou1994121–130-37] The conflicts ended when pretender to the throne [Henry IV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_IV_of_France), after converting to Catholicism to gain entry to the capital, entered the city in 1594 to claim the crown of France. This king made several improvements to the capital during his reign: he completed the construction of Paris's first uncovered, sidewalk-lined bridge, the [Pont Neuf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pont_Neuf), built a Louvre extension connecting it to the [Tuileries Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuileries_Palace), and created the first Paris residential square, the Place Royale, now [Place des Vosges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Place_des_Vosges). Despite Henry IV's efforts to improve city circulation, the narrowness of Paris's streets was a contributing factor in his assassination near [Les Halles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Les_Halles) marketplace in 1610.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFierro1996577-38]

During the 17th century, [Cardinal Richelieu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cardinal_Richelieu), chief minister of [Louis XIII](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_XIII), was determined to make Paris the most beautiful city in Europe. He built five new bridges, a new chapel for the [College of Sorbonne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College_of_Sorbonne), and a palace for himself, the [Palais-Cardinal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palais-Royal). After Richelieu died in 1642, it was renamed the [Palais-Royal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palais-Royal).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFierro1996582-39]



Lutetia Parisiorum vulgo Paris, Plan de Paris en 1657, [Jan Janssonius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jan_Janssonius)

Due to the Parisian uprisings during the [Fronde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fronde) civil war, [Louis XIV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_XIV) moved his court to a new palace, [Versailles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palace_of_Versailles), in 1682. Although no longer the capital of France, arts and sciences in the city flourished with the [Comédie-Française](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Com%C3%A9die-Fran%C3%A7aise), the Academy of Painting, and the [French Academy of Sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Academy_of_Sciences). To demonstrate that the city was safe from attack, the king had the [city walls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_walls_of_Paris) demolished and replaced with tree-lined boulevards that would become the *[Grands Boulevards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boulevards_of_Paris#The_grands_boulevards)*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECombeau200342–43-40] Other marks of his reign were the [Collège des Quatre-Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coll%C3%A8ge_des_Quatre-Nations), the [Place Vendôme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Place_Vend%C3%B4me), the [Place des Victoires](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Place_des_Victoires), and [Les Invalides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Les_Invalides).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFierro1996590–591-41]

Paris has been one of the world's major centres of [finance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finance), [diplomacy](28165911.md), [commerce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commerce), [culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture), [fashion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fashion_capital), and [gastronomy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gastronomy) since the 17th century.[^cite_note-42]

### 18th and 19th centuries

See also: [Paris in the 18th century](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_in_the_18th_century), [Paris during the Second Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_during_the_Second_Empire), and [Haussmann's renovation of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haussmann's_renovation_of_Paris)

Paris grew in population from about 400,000 in 1640 to 650,000 in 1780.[^cite_note-43] A new boulevard named the [Champs-Élysées](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Champs-%C3%89lys%C3%A9es) extended the city west to [Étoile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Place_Charles_de_Gaulle),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECombeau200345–47-44] while the working-class neighbourhood of the [Faubourg Saint-Antoine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faubourg_Saint-Antoine) on the eastern side of the city grew increasingly crowded with poor migrant workers from other regions of France.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESarmant2012129–133-45] Paris's role in [education](379033.md) and [sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Science_and_technology_in_France) led Paris to be known as the City of Light during the [age of Enlightenment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_of_Enlightenment) in the 18th century.[^cite_note-46][^cite_note-47]



The storming of the [Bastille](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bastille) on 14 July 1789, by [Jean-Pierre Houël](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Pierre_Hou%C3%ABl)

In the summer of 1789, Paris became the centre stage of the [French Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Revolution). On 14 July, a mob seized the arsenal at the [Invalides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Les_Invalides), acquiring thousands of guns, with which it [stormed the Bastille](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Storming_of_the_Bastille), a principal symbol of royal authority. The first independent [Paris Commune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_Commune_\(1789%E2%80%931795\)), or city council, met in the *[Hôtel de Ville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H%C3%B4tel_de_Ville,_Paris)* and elected a [Mayor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayor_\(France\)), the astronomer [Jean Sylvain Bailly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean_Sylvain_Bailly), on 15 July.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPaine1998453-48]

[Louis XVI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_XVI) and the royal family were [brought to Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women's_march_on_Versailles) and incarcerated in the Tuileries Palace. In 1793, as the revolution turned increasingly radical, the king, queen, and mayor were beheaded by [guillotine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guillotine) in the [Reign of Terror](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reign_of_Terror), along with more than 16,000 others throughout France.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFierro1996674-49] The property of the aristocracy and the church was [nationalised](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biens_nationaux), and the city's churches were closed, sold, or demolished.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESarmant2012144-50] A succession of revolutionary factions ruled Paris until [9 November 1799](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coup_of_18_Brumaire) (*coup d'état du 18 brumaire*), when [Napoleon Bonaparte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleon_Bonaparte) seized power as First Consul.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESarmant2012147-51]

The population of Paris had dropped by 100,000 during the Revolution, but after 1799 it surged with 160,000 new residents, reaching 660,000 by 1815.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESarmant2012148-52] Napoleon replaced the elected government of Paris with a prefect who reported directly to him. He began erecting monuments to military glory, including the [Arc de Triomphe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arc_de_Triomphe), and improved the neglected infrastructure of the city with new fountains, the [Canal de l'Ourcq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canal_de_l'Ourcq), [Père Lachaise Cemetery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P%C3%A8re_Lachaise_Cemetery) and the city's first metal bridge, the [Pont des Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pont_des_Arts). The [Arc de Triomphe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arc_de_Triomphe) was eventually completed in 1836.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESarmant2012148-52]



The Eiffel Tower, under construction in November 1888, startled Parisians—and the world—with its modernity.

During the [Restoration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bourbon_Restoration_in_France), the bridges and squares of Paris were returned to their pre-Revolution names; the [July Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/July_Revolution) in 1830, commemorated by the [July Column](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/July_Column) on the [Place de la Bastille](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Place_de_la_Bastille), brought to power a constitutional monarch, [Louis Philippe I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_Philippe_I). The first railway line to Paris opened in 1837, beginning a new period of massive migration from the [provinces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Provinces_of_France) to the city.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESarmant2012148-52] In 1848, Louis-Philippe was overthrown by a [popular uprising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Revolution_of_1848) in the streets of Paris. His successor, [Napoleon III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleon_III), alongside the newly appointed prefect of the Seine, [Georges-Eugène Haussmann](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georges-Eug%C3%A8ne_Haussmann), launched a huge public works project to build wide new boulevards, a new opera house, a central market, new aqueducts, sewers and parks, including the [Bois de Boulogne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bois_de_Boulogne) and [Bois de Vincennes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bois_de_Vincennes).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDe_Moncan20127–35-53] In 1860, Napoleon III annexed the surrounding towns and created eight new arrondissements, expanding Paris to its current limits.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDe_Moncan20127–35-53]

During the [Franco-Prussian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franco-Prussian_War) (1870–1871), Paris was besieged by the [Prussian Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prussian_Army). Following several months of blockade, hunger, and then bombardment by the Prussians, the city was forced to surrender on 28 January 1871. After seizing power in Paris on 28 March, a revolutionary government known as the [Paris Commune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_Commune) held power for two months, before being harshly suppressed by the French army during the "[Bloody Week](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semaine_sanglante)" at the end of May 1871.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERougerie2014118-54]

In the late 19th century, Paris hosted two major international expositions: the [1889 Universal Exposition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exposition_Universelle_\(1889\)), which featured the new Eiffel Tower, was held to mark the centennial of the French Revolution; and the [1900 Universal Exposition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exposition_Universelle_\(1900\)) gave Paris the [Pont Alexandre III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pont_Alexandre_III), the [Grand Palais](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Palais), the [Petit Palais](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petit_Palais) and the first [Paris Métro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_M%C3%A9tro) line.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFraserSpalding2011117-55] Paris became the laboratory of [Naturalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naturalism_\(literature\)) ([Émile Zola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89mile_Zola)) and [Symbolism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Symbolism_\(arts\)) ([Charles Baudelaire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Baudelaire) and [Paul Verlaine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Verlaine)), and of [Impressionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impressionism) in art ([Courbet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Courbet), [Manet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manet), [Monet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Claude_Monet), [Renoir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renoir)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFierro1996490–491-56]

### 20th and 21st centuries

See also: [Paris in the Belle Époque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_in_the_Belle_%C3%89poque), [Paris during the First World War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_during_the_First_World_War), [Paris between the Wars (1919–1939)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_between_the_Wars_\(1919%E2%80%931939\)), [Paris in World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_in_World_War_II), and [History of Paris (1946–2000)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Paris_\(1946%E2%80%932000\))

By 1901, the population of Paris had grown to about 2,715,000.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECombeau200361-57] At the beginning of the century, artists from around the world, including [Pablo Picasso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pablo_Picasso), [Modigliani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amedeo_Modigliani), and [Henri Matisse](60203.md) made Paris their home. It was the birthplace of [Fauvism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fauvism), [Cubism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cubism) and [abstract art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abstract_art),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFierro1996497-58][^cite_note-59] and authors such as [Marcel Proust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marcel_Proust) were exploring new approaches to literature.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFierro1996491-60]

During the [First World War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_World_War), Paris sometimes found itself on the front line; 600 to 1,000 Paris taxis played a small but highly important symbolic role in transporting 6,000 soldiers to the front line at the [First Battle of the Marne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Battle_of_the_Marne). The city was also bombed by [Zeppelins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zeppelin) and shelled by German [long-range guns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_Gun).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFierro1996750-61] In the years after the war, known as *[Les Années Folles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_between_the_Wars_\(1919%E2%80%931939\))*, Paris continued to be a mecca for writers, musicians and artists from around the world, including [Ernest Hemingway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernest_Hemingway), [Igor Stravinsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Igor_Stravinsky), [James Joyce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Joyce), [Josephine Baker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Josephine_Baker), [Eva Kotchever](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eva_Kotchever), [Henry Miller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Miller), [Anaïs Nin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ana%C3%AFs_Nin), [Sidney Bechet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sidney_Bechet)[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShack2001-62] and [Salvador Dalí](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salvador_Dal%C3%AD).[^cite_note-Meisler-63]

In the years after the [peace conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_Peace_Conference,_1919), the city was also home to growing numbers of students and activists from [French colonies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_colonial_empire) and other Asian and African countries, who later became leaders of their countries, such as [Ho Chi Minh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ho_Chi_Minh), [Zhou Enlai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhou_Enlai) and [Léopold Sédar Senghor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L%C3%A9opold_S%C3%A9dar_Senghor).[^cite_note-64]



General [Charles de Gaulle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_de_Gaulle) on the Champs-Élysées celebrating the liberation of Paris, 26 August 1944

On 14 June 1940, the German army marched into Paris, which had been declared an "[open city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Open_city)".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESarmant2012217-65] On 16–17 July 1942, following German orders, the French police and gendarmes arrested 12,884 Jews, including 4,115 children, and confined them during five days at the [*Vel d'Hiv*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vel'_d'Hiv_Roundup), from which they were transported by train to the extermination camp at [Auschwitz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auschwitz_concentration_camp). None of the children came back.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFierro1996637-66][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESarmant2012218-67] On 25 August 1944, the city was liberated by the [French 2nd Armoured Division](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2nd_Armored_Division_\(France\)) and the [4th Infantry Division](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/4th_Infantry_Division_\(United_States\)) of the [United States Army](32087.md). General [Charles de Gaulle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_de_Gaulle) led a huge and emotional crowd down the Champs Élysées towards Notre Dame de Paris and made a rousing speech from the [Hôtel de Ville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H%C3%B4tel_de_Ville,_Paris).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFierro1996242–243-68]

Paris became involved in the [Algerian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algerian_War) for independence, which occurred in the 1950s and 1960s; in August 1961, the pro-independence [FLN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Liberation_Front_\(Algeria\)), targeted and killed 11 Paris policemen, leading to the imposition of a curfew on Muslims of Algeria (who, at that time, were French citizens). On 17 October 1961, an unauthorised but peaceful protest demonstration of Algerians against the curfew led to [violent confrontations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1961_Paris_massacre) between the police and demonstrators, in which at least 40 people were killed. The anti-independence [Organisation armée secrète](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organisation_arm%C3%A9e_secr%C3%A8te) (OAS) carried out a series of bombings in Paris throughout 1961 and 1962.[^cite_note-69][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFierro1996658-70]

In May 1968, protesting students occupied the [Sorbonne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sorbonne_\(building\)) and put up barricades in the [Latin Quarter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_Quarter,_Paris). Thousands of Parisian blue-collar workers joined the students, and the movement grew into a two-week general strike. Supporters of the government won the June elections by a large majority. The [May 1968 events in France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/May_1968_events_in_France) resulted in the break-up of the [University of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Paris) into 13 independent campuses.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESarmant2012226-71] In 1975, the National Assembly changed the status of Paris to that of other French cities and, on 25 March 1977, [Jacques Chirac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacques_Chirac) became the first elected mayor of Paris since 1793.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFierro1996260-72]

The [Tour Maine-Montparnasse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tour_Montparnasse), the tallest building in the city at 57 storeys and 210 m (689 ft) high, was built between 1969 and 1973. It was highly controversial, and it remains the only building in the centre of the city over 32 storeys high.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESarmant2012222-73] The population of Paris dropped from 2,850,000 in 1954 to 2,152,000 in 1990, as middle-class families moved to the suburbs.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECombeau2003107–108-74] A suburban railway network, the [RER](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R%C3%A9seau_Express_R%C3%A9gional) (Réseau Express Régional), was built to complement the Métro; the [Périphérique](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P%C3%A9riph%C3%A9rique_\(Paris\)) expressway encircling the city was completed in 1973.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBellde-Shalit2011247-75] The bank of the [Seine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seine) in the city centre, which includes the [Louvre](17546.md) and the Eiffel Tower was classified as a [UNESCO World Heritage Site](44940.md) in 1991.[^cite_note-unesco-76]

Most of the postwar presidents of the [Fifth Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Fifth_Republic) wanted to leave their own monuments in Paris; President [Georges Pompidou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georges_Pompidou) started the [Centre Georges Pompidou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centre_Georges_Pompidou) (1977), [Valéry Giscard d'Estaing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Val%C3%A9ry_Giscard_d'Estaing) began the [Musée d'Orsay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mus%C3%A9e_d'Orsay) (1986); President [François Mitterrand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fran%C3%A7ois_Mitterrand) had the [Opéra Bastille](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Op%C3%A9ra_Bastille) built (1985–1989), the new site of the *[Bibliothèque nationale de France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biblioth%C3%A8que_nationale_de_France)* (1996), the [Arche de la Défense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arche_de_la_D%C3%A9fense) (1985–1989) in [La Défense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_D%C3%A9fense), as well as the [Louvre Pyramid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louvre_Pyramid) with its underground courtyard (1983–1989); [Jacques Chirac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacques_Chirac) (2006), the [Musée du quai Branly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mus%C3%A9e_du_Quai_Branly_%E2%80%93_Jacques_Chirac).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESarmant2012226–230-77]

In the early 21st century, the population of Paris began to increase slowly again, as more young people moved into the city. It reached 2.25 million in 2011. In March 2001, [Bertrand Delanoë](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bertrand_Delano%C3%AB) became the first socialist mayor; he was re-elected in March 2008.[^cite_note-78] Bertrand Delanoë also transformed a section of the highway along the Left Bank of the Seine into an urban promenade and park, the [Promenade des Berges de la Seine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Promenade_des_Berges_de_la_Seine), which he inaugurated in June 2013.[^cite_note-79]



Demonstrators at the [Place de la République](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Place_de_la_R%C3%A9publique), Paris, 11 January 2015, during the [Republican marches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_marches) after the [*Charlie Hebdo* shooting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charlie_Hebdo_shooting)

In 2007, President [Nicolas Sarkozy](557007.md) launched the [Grand Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Paris) project to integrate Paris more closely with the towns in the region around it. After many modifications, the new area, named the [Metropolis of Grand Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Paris), with a population of 6.7 million, was created on 1 January 2016.[^cite_note-Lichfield-80] The City of Paris and the national government approved the plans for the [Grand Paris Express](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Paris_Express) four years later, totalling 205 km (127 mi) of automated metro lines to connect Paris, the innermost three departments around Paris, airports and [high-speed rail (TGV)](57212.md) stations, at an estimated cost of €35 billion.[^cite_note-metro-81] The system is scheduled to be completed by 2030.[^cite_note-82]

A [series of attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/January_2015_%C3%8Ele-de-France_attacks) occurred in the Paris region, especially in Paris, in January 2015, the most serious being the [shooting at the offices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charlie_Hebdo_shooting) of [Charlie Hebdo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charlie_Hebdo). 17 people died in the attacks, which [Al-Qaeda in the Arabian Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Qaeda_in_the_Arabian_Peninsula) claimed responsibility.[^cite_note-83][^cite_note-84] [1.5 million people marched in Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_marches) in a show of solidarity against terrorism and in support of freedom of speech.[^cite_note-85] [Terrorist attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/November_2015_Paris_attacks), claimed by [ISIL](9087364.md),[^cite_note-86] occurred again in November, which killed 130 people and injured more than 350.[^cite_note-87]

In an aim to limit the [effects of climate change](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Effects_of_climate_change) below 2 °C, the [Paris Agreement](30242372.md) was signed by 196 nations of the [United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change](31898.md) on 22 April 2016 in Paris.[^cite_note-88] The following year, billionaire [François Pinault](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fran%C3%A7ois_Pinault) received a 50‑year lease to convert the historic [Bourse de Commerce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bourse_de_commerce_\(Paris\)) into a contemporary art museum, overseen by [Japanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_people) architect [Tadao Ando](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tadao_Ando).[^cite_note-89] Construction was delayed by the [COVID-19 pandemic](62750956.md), reopening in May 2021.[^cite_note-90] The [Notre Dame Cathedral caught fire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Notre-Dame_fire) on 15 April 2019, beginning in the eaves of its roof,[^cite_note-Notre-Dame-91] engulfing the [spire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spire) and most of the roof. Firefighters were able to control the blaze, saving the main structure including the [bell towers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bell_tower) and [rose windows](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rose_window), and there were no injuries;[^cite_note-Notre-Dame-91] [it was reopened](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reopening_of_Notre-Dame_de_Paris) on 7 December 2024.[^cite_note-92]

In terms of international connectedness, as of 2024, Paris was one of eight cities worldwide that was classified as an "Alpha+" city by the [Globalization and World Cities Research Network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Globalization_and_World_Cities_Research_Network).[^cite_note-93]

## Geography

Main articles: [Geography of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Paris) and [Climate of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_of_Paris)



A 2022 satellite image of Paris, by ESA's [Sentinel-2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sentinel-2) mission

Paris is located in northern central France, in a north-bending arc of the river [Seine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seine), whose crest includes two islands, the [Île Saint-Louis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%8Ele_Saint-Louis) and the larger [Île de la Cité](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%8Ele_de_la_Cit%C3%A9), which form the oldest part of Paris. The river's mouth on the [English Channel](9230.md) (*La Manche*) is about 233 mi (375 km) downstream from Paris. Paris is spread widely on both banks of the river.[^cite_note-City-94] Overall, Paris is relatively flat, and the lowest point is 35 m (115 ft) [above sea level](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Above_mean_sea_level). Paris has several prominent hills, the highest of which is [Montmartre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montmartre) at 130 m (427 ft).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlackmoreMcConnachie2004153-95]

Excluding the outlying parks of [Bois de Boulogne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bois_de_Boulogne) and [Bois de Vincennes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bois_de_Vincennes), Paris covers an oval measuring about 87 km<sup>2</sup> (34 sq mi) in area, enclosed by the 35 km (22 mi) ring road, the [Boulevard Périphérique](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boulevard_P%C3%A9riph%C3%A9rique).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELawrenceGondrand201069-96] Paris's last major annexation of outlying territories in 1860 gave it its modern form, and created the 20 clockwise-spiralling arrondissements (municipal boroughs). From the 1860 area of 78 km<sup>2</sup> (30 sq mi), the city limits were expanded marginally to 86.9 km<sup>2</sup> (33.6 sq mi) in the 1920s. In 1929, the Bois de Boulogne and Bois de Vincennes forest parks were annexed to the city, bringing its area to about 105 km<sup>2</sup> (41 sq mi).[^cite_note-97] The metropolitan area is 2,300 km<sup>2</sup> (890 sq mi).[^cite_note-City-94]

### Climate



Autumn in [Avenue Raphaël](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avenue_Rapha%C3%ABl?action=edit\&redlink=1), Paris

Paris has a [Oceanic climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oceanic_climate) ([Köppen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification): *Cfb*),[^cite_note-98] typical of western Europe.[^cite_note-99] This climate type features cool winters and warm summers, with average temperatures between 15 and 25 °C (59 and 77 °F) in July.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELawrenceGondrand2010309-100][^cite_note-101] Each year there are a few days when the temperature rises above 32 °C (90 °F). Longer periods of more intense heat sometimes occur, such as the [heat wave of 2003](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2003_European_heat_wave), when temperatures exceeded 30 °C (86 °F) for weeks, reached 40 °C (104 °F) on some days, and rarely cooled down at night.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoldstein20058-102]

The weather in spring and autumn is generally mild during the day and cool at night, but is unstable.[^cite_note-103] In winter, sunshine is scarce, days are cool, and nights are cold but generally above freezing, with low temperatures around 3 °C (37 °F).[^cite_note-104] Light night frosts are quite common, but the temperature seldom dips below −5 °C (23 °F). Paris sometimes sees light snow or [flurries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Snow_flurry) with or without accumulation.[^cite_note-105] In summer, the weather is warm, averaging 22 °C (72 °F), and there is 8 hours of sunshine on average per day.[^cite_note-106]

Paris has an average annual precipitation of 641 mm (25.2 in), and experiences light rainfall distributed evenly throughout the year. As of 2025, the highest recorded temperature was 42.6 °C (108.7 °F), on 25 July 2019.[^cite_note-107] The lowest was −23.9 °C (−11.0 °F), on 10 December 1879.[^cite_note-climate-108]

## Administration

Main article: [Administration of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Administration_of_Paris)

### City government

Further information: [Arrondissements of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arrondissements_of_Paris)

See also: [Mayor of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayor_of_Paris)



A map of the [arrondissements of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arrondissements_of_Paris)

For almost all of its long history, except for a few brief periods, Paris was governed directly by representatives of the king, emperor, or president of France. In 1974, Paris was granted municipal autonomy by the National Assembly.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFierro1996334-112] The first modern elected mayor of Paris was [Jacques Chirac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacques_Chirac), elected in March 1977, becoming the city's first mayor since 1871 and only the fourth since 1794. The current mayor is [Emmanuel Grégoire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emmanuel_Gr%C3%A9goire), a member of the [Socialist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_Party_\(France\)), who was elected in [2026](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_Paris_municipal_election).[^cite_note-113]



The [Hôtel de Ville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H%C3%B4tel_de_Ville,_Paris), or city hall

The mayor of Paris is [elected indirectly](652583.md) by Paris voters. The voters of each of the city's 20 arrondissements elect members to the *[Conseil de Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conseil_de_Paris)* (Council of Paris), which elects the mayor. The council is composed of 163 members. Each arrondissement is allocated many seats, dependent upon its population, from 10 members for each of the least-populated arrondissements to 34 members for the most populated. The council is elected using [closed list](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Closed_list) [proportional representation](23298.md) in a [two-round system](25932.md).[^cite_note-Election_Code_–_Article_L260-114]

Party lists winning an absolute [majority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majority) in the first round – or at least a [plurality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plurality_\(voting\)) in the second round – automatically win half the seats of an arrondissement. The remaining half of the seats are distributed proportionally to all lists which win at least 5% of the vote, using the [highest averages method](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Highest_averages_method).[^cite_note-Election_Code_–_Article_L260-114] This ensures that the winning party or coalition always wins a majority of the seats, even if they do not win an absolute majority of the vote.[^cite_note-Pariscouncil-115]

Before the [2020 Paris municipal election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Paris_municipal_election), each of Paris's 20 arrondissements had its own town hall and a directly elected council (*[conseil d'arrondissement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conseil_d'arrondissement)*), which elects an arrondissement mayor.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShales200716-116] The council of each arrondissement is composed of members of the Conseil de Paris, and members who serve only on the council of the arrondissement. The number of deputy mayors in each arrondissement varies depending on its population. As of 1996, there were 20 arrondissement mayors and 120 deputy mayors.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFierro1996334-112] The creation of [Paris Centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_Centre), a unified administrative division with a single mayor covering the first four arrondissements, took effect with the said 2020 election. The other 16 arrondissements continue to have their own mayors.[^cite_note-117]

### Métropole du Grand Paris



A map of the Greater Paris Metropolis and its governing territories

In January 2016, the [Métropole du Grand Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Paris) came into existence.[^cite_note-MGP-118] It is an administrative structure for co-operation between the City of Paris and its nearest suburbs. It includes the City of Paris, plus the communes of the three departments of the inner suburbs, [Hauts-de-Seine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hauts-de-Seine), [Seine-Saint-Denis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seine-Saint-Denis) and [Val-de-Marne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Val-de-Marne), plus seven communes in the outer suburbs, including [Argenteuil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Argenteuil) in [Val d'Oise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Val_d'Oise) and [Paray-Vieille-Poste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paray-Vieille-Poste) in [Essonne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Essonne), which were added to include the major airports of Paris. The Metropole covers 814 km<sup>2</sup> (314 sq mi). In 2015, it had a population of 6.945 million people.[^cite_note-119][^cite_note-lemoniteur1-120]

The new structure is administered by a Metropolitan Council of 210 members, not directly elected, but chosen by the councils of the member Communes. By 2020, its basic competencies will include urban planning, housing, and protection of the environment.[^cite_note-MGP-118][^cite_note-lemoniteur1-120] In January 2016, [Patrick Ollier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patrick_Ollier) was elected the first president of the metropolitan council. Though the Metropole has a population of nearly seven million people and accounts for 25 percent of the GDP of France, it has a very small budget: just 65 million Euros, compared with eight billion Euros for the City of Paris.[^cite_note-121]

### Regional government

The [Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Region) of [Île de France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%8Ele_de_France), including Paris and its surrounding communities, is governed by the [Regional Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regional_Council_of_%C3%8Ele-de-France), composed of 209 members representing the different communes. In December 2015, a list of candidates of the Union of the Right, a coalition of centrist and right-wing parties, led by [Valérie Pécresse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Val%C3%A9rie_P%C3%A9cresse), narrowly won the regional election, defeating a coalition of Socialists and ecologists. The Socialists had governed the region for seventeen years. The regional council has 121 members from the Union of the Right, 66 from the Union of the Left and 22 from the extreme right National Front.[^cite_note-122]

### National government



The [Élysée Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89lys%C3%A9e_Palace), official residence of the [President of France](24899.md)

As the capital of France, Paris is the seat of France's [national government](3398091.md). For the executive, the two chief officers each have their own official residences, which also serve as their offices. The [President of the French Republic](24899.md) resides at the [Élysée Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89lys%C3%A9e_Palace).[^cite_note-123] The [Prime Minister](218751.md)'s seat is at the [Hôtel Matignon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H%C3%B4tel_Matignon).[^cite_note-124][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKnappWright200693–94-125] Government ministries are located in various parts of the city, many near the Hôtel Matignon.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBorrus2012288-126]

Both houses of the [French Parliament](1009776.md) are located on the Rive Gauche. The upper house, the [Senate](567323.md), meets in the [Palais du Luxembourg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palais_du_Luxembourg). The more important lower house, the [National Assembly](299616.md), meets in the [Palais Bourbon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palais_Bourbon). The [President of the Senate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_presidents_of_the_Senate_of_France), the second-highest public official in France, with the President of the Republic being the sole superior, resides in the [Petit Luxembourg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petit_Luxembourg), a smaller palace annexe to the Palais du Luxembourg.[^cite_note-127]



The Palais-Royal, residence of the [Conseil d'État](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conseil_d'%C3%89tat_\(France\))

France's highest courts are located in Paris. The [Court of Cassation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Court_of_Cassation_\(France\)), the highest court in the judicial order, which reviews criminal and civil cases, is located in the [Palais de Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palais_de_Justice,_Paris) on the *Île de la Cité*.[^cite_note-128] The [Conseil d'État](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_State_\(France\)), which provides legal advice to the executive and acts as the highest court in the administrative order, judging litigation against public bodies, is located in the [Palais-Royal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palais-Royal) in the [1st arrondissement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1st_arrondissement_of_Paris).[^cite_note-129] The [Constitutional Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_Council_of_France), an advisory body with ultimate authority on the constitutionality of laws and government decrees, meets in the Montpensier wing of the Palais Royal.[^cite_note-130]

Paris and its region host the headquarters of several international organisations, including [UNESCO](21786641.md), the [Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organisation_for_Economic_Co-operation_and_Development), the [International Chamber of Commerce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Chamber_of_Commerce), the [Paris Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_Club), the [European Space Agency](10363.md), the [International Energy Agency](285292.md), the *[Organisation internationale de la Francophonie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organisation_internationale_de_la_Francophonie)*, the [European Union Institute for Security Studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Union_Institute_for_Security_Studies), the [International Bureau of Weights and Measures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Bureau_of_Weights_and_Measures), the [International Exhibition Bureau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bureau_of_International_Expositions), and the [International Federation for Human Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Federation_for_Human_Rights).

### Police force



[Gendarmerie](6330819.md) motorcyclists

The security of Paris is mainly the responsibility of the [Prefecture of Police of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prefecture_of_Police_of_Paris), a subdivision of the [Ministry of the Interior](871703.md). It supervises the units of the [National Police](614023.md) who patrol the city and the three neighbouring departments. It is also responsible for providing emergency services, including the [Paris Fire Brigade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_Fire_Brigade). Its headquarters is on [Place Louis Lépine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Place_Louis_L%C3%A9pine) on the [Île de la Cité](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%8Ele_de_la_Cit%C3%A9).[^cite_note-Presentation_Generale-131]

There are 43,800 officers under the prefecture, and a fleet of more than 6,000 vehicles, including police cars, motorcycles, fire trucks, boats, and helicopters.[^cite_note-Presentation_Generale-131] The national police has its own special unit for riot control and crowd control, and security of public buildings, called the [Compagnies Républicaines de Sécurité](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Compagnies_R%C3%A9publicaines_de_S%C3%A9curit%C3%A9) (CRS). Vans of CRS agents are frequently seen in the centre of Paris when there are demonstrations and public events. The police are supported by the [National Gendarmerie](6330819.md), a branch of the [French Armed Forces](10724.md). Their police operations are supervised by the [Ministry of the Interior](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interior_ministry).[^cite_note-132]

Crime in Paris is similar to that in most large cities. Violent crime is relatively rare in the city centre. Political violence is uncommon, though very large demonstrations may occur in Paris and other French cities simultaneously. These demonstrations, usually managed by a strong police presence, can turn confrontational and escalate into violence.[^cite_note-133]

## Cityscape



A panorama of Paris from the Eiffel Tower, in a 360-degree view. The [Seine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seine) river flows from the north-east to the south-west, right to left

### Urbanism and architecture

See also: [Architecture of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_Paris), [Haussmann's renovation of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haussmann's_renovation_of_Paris), [Religious buildings in Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_buildings_in_Paris), and [List of tallest buildings in the Paris region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_tallest_buildings_in_the_Paris_region)



The [Rue de Rivoli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rue_de_Rivoli)



The [Place des Vosges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Place_des_Vosges), the oldest planned square in Paris

Paris is one of the few world capitals that has rarely seen destruction by catastrophe or war. As a result, even its earliest history is visible in its street map, and centuries of rulers adding their respective architectural marks on the capital have resulted in an accumulated wealth of history-rich monuments and buildings whose beauty plays a large part in giving Paris the reputation it has today.[^cite_note-134] At its origin, before the Middle Ages, Paris was composed of several islands and sandbanks in a bend of the [Seine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seine). Of those, two remain today: [Île Saint-Louis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%8Ele_Saint-Louis) and the [Île de la Cité](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%8Ele_de_la_Cit%C3%A9). A third one is the 1827 artificially created [Île aux Cygnes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%8Ele_aux_Cygnes).

Modern Paris owes much of its downtown plan and architectural harmony to [Napoleon III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleon_III) and his Prefect of the Seine, [Baron Haussmann](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georges-Eug%C3%A8ne_Haussmann). Between [1853 and 1870 they rebuilt the city centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haussmann's_renovation_of_Paris), created the wide downtown boulevards and squares where the boulevards intersected, imposed standard facades along the boulevards, and required that the facades be built of the distinctive cream-grey "[Paris stone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_stone)". They built the major parks around central Paris.[^cite_note-135] The high residential population of the city centre makes Paris much different from most other major western cities.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBraimohVlek200812-136]

Paris's urbanism laws have been under strict control since the early 17th century,[^cite_note-plan_hauteurs-137] particularly where street-front alignment, building height and building distribution is concerned.[^cite_note-plan_hauteurs-137] The 210 m (690 ft) [Tour Montparnasse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tour_Montparnasse) was both Paris's and France's tallest building from 1973 until 2011,[^cite_note-138] when the record was taken by the [La Défense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_D%C3%A9fense) district's [Tour First](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tour_First) in the commune of [Courbevoie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Courbevoie).

### Housing



[Front de Seine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Front_de_Seine) development along the river [Seine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seine)

In 2018, the most expensive residential street in Paris by average price per square metre was [Avenue Montaigne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avenue_Montaigne), at 22,372 euros per square metre.[^cite_note-139] In 2011, the number of residences in the City of Paris was 1,356,074. Among these, 1,165,541 (85.9 percent) were main residences, 91,835 (6.8 percent) were secondary residences, and the remaining 7.3 percent were empty.[^cite_note-insee_logement-140]

Sixty-two percent of buildings date from 1949 and before, with 20 percent built between 1949 and 1974. 18 percent of Paris buildings were built after 1974.[^cite_note-notaires_idf-141] Two-thirds of the city's 1.3 million residences are studio and two-room apartments. Paris averages 1.9 people per residence, a number that has remained constant since the 1980s, which is less than Île-de-France's 2.33 people-per-residence average. Only 33 percent of Parisian principal residents own their habitation, against 47 percent for the wider [Île-de-France](1716427.md) region. Most of Paris's population rents their residence.[^cite_note-notaires_idf-141] In 2017, social or public housing was 19.9 percent of Paris's residences. Its distribution varies widely throughout Paris, from 2.6 percent of the housing in the wealthy 7th arrondissement to 39.9 percent in the 19th arrondissement.[^cite_note-142]

In February 2019, a Paris NGO conducted its annual citywide count of homeless persons. They counted 3,641 homeless persons in Paris, of whom twelve percent were women. More than half had been homeless for more than a year. 2,885 were living in the streets or parks, 298 in train and metro stations, and 756 in other forms of temporary shelter. This was an increase of 588 persons since 2018.[^cite_note-143]

### Suburbs



Western Paris in 2016, photographed by a [SkySat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SkySat) satellite



West of Paris seen from [Tour Montparnasse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tour_Montparnasse), 2019

Aside from the 20th-century addition of the [Bois de Boulogne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bois_de_Boulogne), the [Bois de Vincennes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bois_de_Vincennes), and the Paris heliport, Paris's administrative limits have remained unchanged since 1860. A greater administrative [Seine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seine_\(department\)) department had been governing Paris and its suburbs since its creation in 1790, but the rising suburban population had made it difficult to maintain as a unique entity. To address this problem, the parent "District de la région parisienne" ('district of the Paris region') was reorganised into several new departments from 1968: Paris became a department in itself, and the administration of its suburbs was divided between the three new departments surrounding it. The district of the Paris region was renamed "[Île-de-France](1716427.md)" in 1977, but this abbreviated "Paris region" name is still commonly used today to describe the Île-de-France, and as a vague reference to the entire Paris agglomeration.[^cite_note-144] Long-intended measures to unite Paris with its suburbs began in January 2016, when the Métropole du [Grand Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Paris) came into existence.[^cite_note-MGP-118]

Paris's disconnect with its suburbs, its lack of suburban transportation, in particular, became all too apparent with the Paris agglomeration's growth. [Paul Delouvrier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Delouvrier) promised to resolve the Paris-suburbs *mésentente* when he became head of the Paris region in 1961.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMasson1984536-145] Two of his most ambitious projects for the Region were the construction of five suburban "villes nouvelles" ("new cities")[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEYarri2008407-146] and the [RER](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R%C3%A9seau_Express_R%C3%A9gional) commuter train network.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGordon200646–47-147]

Many other suburban residential districts (*grands ensembles*) were built between the 1960s and 1970s to provide a low-cost solution for a rapidly expanding population.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECastells198375-148] These districts were socially mixed at first,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETomasBlancBonillaIERP2003237-149] but few residents actually owned their homes. The growing economy made these accessible to the middle classes only from the 1970s.[^cite_note-villes_nouvelles-150] Their poor construction quality and their haphazard insertion into existing urban growth contributed to their desertion by those able to move elsewhere, and their repopulation by those with more limited resources.[^cite_note-villes_nouvelles-150]

These areas, *quartiers sensibles* ("sensitive quarters"), are in northern and eastern Paris, namely around its [Goutte d'Or](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goutte_d'Or) and [Belleville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belleville,_Paris) neighbourhoods. To the north of Paris, they are grouped mainly in the [Seine-Saint-Denis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seine-Saint-Denis) [department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_\(France\)), and to a lesser extent, to the east in the [Val-d'Oise](90616.md) [department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_\(France\)). Other difficult areas are located in the [Seine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seine) valley, in [Évry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89vry_\(Essonne\)) et [Corbeil-Essonnes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corbeil-Essonnes) ([Essonne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Essonne_\(d%C3%A9partement\))), in [Mureaux](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Les_Mureaux), [Mantes-la-Jolie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mantes-la-Jolie) ([Yvelines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yvelines)), and scattered among social housing districts created by [Delouvrier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Delouvrier)'s 1961 "ville nouvelle" political initiative.[^cite_note-151]

The Paris agglomeration's [urban sociology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_sociology) is basically that of 19th-century Paris: the wealthy live in the west and southwest, and the middle-to-working classes are in the north and east. The remaining areas are mostly middle-class, dotted with wealthy islands in areas of historical importance, namely [Saint-Maur-des-Fossés](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint-Maur-des-Foss%C3%A9s) to the east and [Enghien-les-Bains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enghien-les-Bains) to the north of Paris.[^cite_note-152]

The population of the City of Paris was 2,102,650 in January 2023, down from 2,165,423 in January 2022. Between 2013 and 2023, the population fell by 122,919, or about five percent. The Mayor of Paris, [Anne Hidalgo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anne_Hidalgo), declared that this illustrated the "de-densification" of the city, creating more green space and less crowding.[^cite_note-155][^cite_note-156] Despite the drop, Paris remains the most densely populated city in Europe, with 252 residents per hectare, not counting parks.[^cite_note-Le_Monde,_22_January_2019-157] This drop was attributed partly to a lower birth rate, the departure of middle-class residents, and the possible loss of housing in Paris, due to short-term rentals for tourism.[^cite_note-158]

Paris is the fourth largest municipality in the European Union, after [Berlin](3354.md), [Madrid](41188263.md) and [Rome](25458.md), and is the [34th most densely populated city in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_proper_by_population_density) as of 2020.[^cite_note-worldatlas-pop-159] [Eurostat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurostat) places Paris (6.5 million people) behind London (8 million) and ahead of Berlin (3.5 million), based on the 2012 populations of what Eurostat calls "urban audit core cities".[^cite_note-160] The population of Paris today is lower than its historical peak of 2.9 million in 1921.[^cite_note-161] The principal reasons are a significant decline in household size and a dramatic migration of residents to the suburbs between 1962 and 1975. Factors in the migration included [de-industrialisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/De-industrialisation), high rent, the [gentrification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gentrification) of many inner quarters, the transformation of living space into offices, and greater affluence among working families. Paris's population loss came to a temporary halt at the beginning of the 21st century. The population increased from 2,125,246 in 1999 to 2,240,621 in 2012, before declining again between 2012 and 2023.[^cite_note-162][^cite_note-pophis-163]

Paris is the core of a built-up area that extends well beyond its limits: commonly referred to as the *agglomération Parisienne*, and statistically as a *[unité urbaine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unit%C3%A9_urbaine)* (a measure of [urban area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_area)), the Paris agglomeration's population of 11,013,774 in 2023 made it the [largest urban area in the European Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_urban_areas_in_the_European_Union).[^cite_note-Comparateur_de_territoires.INSEE-4] City-influenced commuter activity reaches further, in a statistical [*aire d'attraction* de Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_Metropolitan_Area), ("functional area", a statistical method comparable to a [metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_area)),[^cite_note-FU-164] that had a population of 13,320,752 in 2023,[^cite_note-Comparateur_de_territoires.INSEE-4] 19.6% of the population of France,[^cite_note-165] and the [largest metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_metropolitan_areas_in_Europe) in the [Eurozone](184391.md).[^cite_note-urbaneurope-166]

In 2012, the Commune of Paris was the most densely populated city in the European Union. There were 21,616 people per square kilometre within the city limits, the NUTS-3 statistical area, ahead of Inner London West, which had 10,374 people per square kilometre. In the same census, three departments bordering Paris, [Hauts-de-Seine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hauts-de-Seine), [Seine-Saint-Denis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seine-Saint-Denis) and [Val-de-Marne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Val-de-Marne), had population densities of over 10,000 people per square kilometre, ranking among the 10 most densely populated areas of the EU.[^cite_note-167]

### Migration

Under French law, people born in foreign countries with no French citizenship at birth are defined as immigrants. In the 2012 census, 135,853 residents of the City of Paris were immigrants from [Europe](9239.md), 112,369 were immigrants from the [Maghreb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maghreb), 70,852 from [sub-Saharan Africa](27067.md) and [Egypt](8087628.md), 5,059 from [Turkey](11125639.md), 91,297 from [Asia](689.md) outside Turkey, 38,858 from the [Americas](29833.md), and 1,365 from the [South Pacific](22621.md).[^cite_note-168]

In the Paris Region, 590,504 residents were immigrants from Europe, 627,078 were immigrants from the Maghreb, 435,339 from sub-Saharan Africa and Egypt, 69,338 from Turkey, 322,330 from Asia outside Turkey, 113,363 from the Americas, and 2,261 from the South Pacific.[^cite_note-169]

In 2012, there were 8,810 British citizens and 10,019 United States citizens living in the City of Paris (Ville de Paris), and 20,466 British citizens and 16,408 United States citizens living in the entire Paris Region ([Île-de-France](1716427.md)).[^cite_note-170][^cite_note-171]

In 2020–2021, about 6 million people, or 41% of the population of the Paris Region, were either immigrants (21%) or had at least one immigrant parent (20%). These figures do not include French people born in Overseas France and their direct descendants.[^cite_note-172]

### Religion

See also: [Religious buildings in Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_buildings_in_Paris)



[Sacré-Cœur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacr%C3%A9-C%C5%93ur,_Paris) in [Montmartre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montmartre)

At the beginning of the twentieth century, Paris was the largest [Catholic](606848.md) city in the world.[^cite_note-173] French census data does not contain information about religious affiliation.[^cite_note-174] In a 2011 survey by the [Institut français d'opinion publique](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institut_fran%C3%A7ais_d'opinion_publique) (IFOP), a French public opinion research organisation, 61 percent of residents of the Paris Region (Île-de-France) identified themselves as [Roman Catholic](606848.md). In the same survey, 7 percent of residents identified themselves as Muslims, 4 percent as Protestants, 2 percent as Jewish, and 25 percent as without religion.

According to the INSEE, between 4 and 5 million French residents were born, or had at least one parent born, in a predominantly [Muslim country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_France), particularly [Algeria](358.md), [Morocco](19291.md), and [Tunisia](30188.md). An IFOP survey in 2008 reported that, of immigrants from these predominantly Muslim countries, 25 percent went to the mosque regularly. 41 percent practised the religion, and 34 percent were believers, but did not practice the religion.[^cite_note-175][^cite_note-176] In 2012 and 2013, it was estimated that there were almost 500,000 Muslims in the City of Paris, 1.5 million Muslims in the Île-de-France region and 4 to 5 million Muslims in France.[^cite_note-177][^cite_note-178]

In 2014, the Jewish population of the Paris Region was estimated to be 282,000, the largest concentration of Jews in the world outside of [Israel](9282173.md) and the United States.[^cite_note-179]

## Economy

Main article: [Economy of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Paris)



[La Défense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_D%C3%A9fense), the largest dedicated business district in Europe, located to the west of Paris

[^cite_note-France.fr-180]



The [headquarters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Headquarters) of [BNP Paribas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BNP_Paribas), the largest banking group in Europe, in the [Boulevard des Italiens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boulevard_des_Italiens)

[^cite_note-181]



[Axa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Axa) headquarters at Hôtel de La Vaupalière



The [Crédit Agricole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cr%C3%A9dit_Agricole) headquarters in [Montrouge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montrouge), south of Paris

[^cite_note-sieges-182]

The economy of the City of Paris is based largely on services and commerce. Of the 390,480 enterprises in Paris, 80.6 percent are engaged in commerce, transportation, and diverse services, 6.5 percent in construction, and 3.8 percent in industry.[^cite_note-183] The story is similar in the [Paris Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_Region) (Île-de-France): 76.7 percent of enterprises are engaged in commerce and services, and 3.4 percent in industry.[^cite_note-184]

At the 2012 census, 59.5% of jobs in the Paris Region were in market services (12.0% in wholesale and retail trade, 9.7% in professional, scientific, and technical services, 6.5% in information and communication, 6.5% in transportation and warehousing, 5.9% in finance and insurance, 5.8% in administrative and support services, 4.6% in accommodation and food services, and 8.5% in various other market services), 26.9% in non-market services (10.4% in human health and social work activities, 9.6% in public administration and defence, and 6.9% in education), 8.2% in manufacturing and utilities (6.6% in manufacturing and 1.5% in utilities), 5.2% in construction, and 0.2% in agriculture.[^cite_note-185][^cite_note-186]

The Paris Region had 5.4 million salaried employees in 2010, of whom 2.2 million were concentrated in 39 *pôles d'emplois* or business districts. The largest of these by number of employees is known in French as the QCA, or *quartier central des affaires*. In 2010, it was the workplace of 500,000 salaried employees, about 30 percent of the salaried employees in Paris and 10 percent of those in the Île-de-France. The largest sectors of activity in the central business district were finance and insurance (16 percent of employees in the district) and business services (15 percent). The district includes a large concentration of department stores, shopping areas, hotels and restaurants, as well a government offices and ministries.[^cite_note-INSEE-187]

The second-largest business district in terms of employment is [La Défense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_D%C3%A9fense), just west of the city. In 2010, it was the workplace of 144,600 employees, of whom 38 percent worked in finance and insurance, 16 percent in business support services. Two other important districts, [Neuilly-sur-Seine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neuilly-sur-Seine) and [Levallois-Perret](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levallois-Perret), are extensions of the Paris business district and of La Défense. Another district, including [Boulogne-Billancourt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boulogne-Billancourt), [Issy-les-Moulineaux](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Issy-les-Moulineaux) and the southern part of the 15th arrondissement, is a centre of activity for the media and information technology.[^cite_note-INSEE-187]

In 2021, the top French companies listed in the [*Fortune* Global 500](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fortune_Global_500) all have their headquarters in the Paris Region. Six are in the central business district of the City of Paris, four are close to the city in the [Hauts-de-Seine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hauts-de-Seine) Department, three are in [La Défense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_D%C3%A9fense), and one is in [Boulogne-Billancourt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boulogne-Billancourt). Some companies, like [Société Générale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soci%C3%A9t%C3%A9_G%C3%A9n%C3%A9rale), have offices in both Paris and [La Défense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_D%C3%A9fense). The Paris Region is France's leading region for economic activity, with a [GDP](12594.md) of [€](9472.md)865 billion, of which €280 billion was in Paris city.[^cite_note-188] In 2021, its GDP ranked first among the metropolitan regions of the EU, and its per-capita GDP PPP was the 8th highest.[^cite_note-189][^cite_note-190][^cite_note-McKinsey-191] While the Paris region's population accounted for 18.8 percent of metropolitan France in 2019,[^cite_note-Pop.Paris-5] the Paris region's GDP accounted for 32 percent of metropolitan France's GDP.[^cite_note-192][^cite_note-193]
