The Paris Region economy has gradually shifted from industry to high-value-added service industries ([finance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Financial_services), IT services) and high-tech manufacturing (electronics, optics, aerospace, etc.).[^cite_note-INSEE_201210-194] The Paris region's most intense economic activity through the central [Hauts-de-Seine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hauts-de-Seine) department and suburban La Défense business district places Paris's economic centre to the west of the city, in a triangle between the *[Opéra Garnier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palais_Garnier)*, *La Défense* and the *[Val de Seine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Val_de_Seine)*.[^cite_note-INSEE_201210-194] While the Paris economy is dominated by [services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Service_Sector), and employment in manufacturing sector has declined sharply, the region remains an important manufacturing centre, particularly for aeronautics, automobiles, and eco industries.[^cite_note-INSEE_201210-194]

In the 2017 worldwide cost of living survey by the [Economist Intelligence Unit](15303116.md), based on a survey made in September 2016, Paris ranked as the seventh most expensive city in the world, and the second most expensive in Europe.[^cite_note-195] In 2018, Paris was the most expensive city in the world along with [Singapore](27318.md) and [Hong Kong](13404.md).[^cite_note-196] [Station F](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Station_F) is a [business incubator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Business_incubator) for startups, noted as the world's largest startup facility.[^cite_note-197] In 2022, Paris was the city with the ninth-highest cost of living in the world.[^cite_note-198]

### Employment and income



The median income in Paris and its nearest departments, 2018. High income in red, low income in yellow.

In 2007, the majority of Paris's salaried employees filled 370,000 businesses services jobs, concentrated in the north-western 8th, 16th and 17th arrondissements.[^cite_note-AlapageINSEE-199] Paris's financial service companies are concentrated in the central-western 8th and 9th arrondissement banking and insurance district.[^cite_note-AlapageINSEE-199] Paris's department store district in the 1st, 6th, 8th, and 9th arrondissements employs ten percent of mostly female Paris workers, with 100,000 of these in the retail trade.[^cite_note-AlapageINSEE-199] Fourteen percent of Parisians worked in hotels and restaurants and other services to individuals.[^cite_note-AlapageINSEE-199]

Nineteen percent of Paris employees work for the State in either administration or education. The majority of Paris's healthcare and social workers work at the hospitals and social housing, concentrated in the peripheral 13th, 14th, 18th, 19th, and 20th arrondissements.[^cite_note-AlapageINSEE-199] Outside Paris, the western Hauts-de-Seine department [La Défense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_D%C3%A9fense) district specialising in finance, insurance, and scientific research, employs 144,600.[^cite_note-INSEE_201210-194] The north-eastern Seine-Saint-Denis audiovisual sector has 200 media firms and 10 major film studios.[^cite_note-INSEE_201210-194]

Paris's manufacturing is mostly focused in its suburbs. Paris has around 75,000 manufacturing workers, most of whom are in the textile, clothing, leather goods, and shoe trades.[^cite_note-INSEE_201210-194] In 2015, the Paris region's 800 aerospace companies employed 100,000.[^cite_note-INSEE_201210-194] Four hundred automobile industry companies employ another 100,000 workers. Many of these are centred in the [Yvelines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yvelines) department, around the Renault and PSA-Citroën plants. This department alone employs 33,000.[^cite_note-INSEE_201210-194] In 2014, the industry as a whole suffered a major loss, with the closing of a major [Aulnay-sous-Bois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aulnay-sous-Bois) Citroën assembly plant.[^cite_note-INSEE_201210-194]

The southern [Essonne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Essonne) department specialises in science and technology.[^cite_note-INSEE_201210-194] The south-eastern [Val-de-Marne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Val-de-Marne), with its wholesale [Rungis food market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/March%C3%A9_international_de_Rungis), specialises in food processing and beverages.[^cite_note-INSEE_201210-194] The Paris region's manufacturing decline is quickly being replaced by eco-industries. These employ about 100,000 workers.[^cite_note-INSEE_201210-194]

Incomes are higher in the Western part of Paris and in the western suburbs, than in the northern and eastern parts of the urban area.[^cite_note-200] While Paris has some of the richest neighbourhoods in France, it also has some of the poorest, mostly on the eastern side of the city. In 2012, 14 percent of households in Paris earned less than €977 per month, the official [poverty line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poverty_line). Twenty-five percent of residents in the 19th arrondissement lived below the poverty line. In Paris's wealthiest neighbourhood, the 7th arrondissement, 7 percent lived below the poverty line.[^cite_note-201] The unemployment rate in Paris in the 4th trimester of 2021 was six percent, compared with 7.4 percent in the whole of France. This was the lowest rate in thirteen years.[^cite_note-202][^cite_note-203]

### Tourism

Main article: [Tourism in Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_in_Paris)

Further information: [Landmarks in Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landmarks_in_Paris), [Historical quarters of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historical_quarters_of_Paris), and [List of tourist attractions in Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_tourist_attractions_in_Paris)



The [Louvre](17546.md), the [most-visited art museum in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_most-visited_art_museums)

As of 2024, Paris was considered the most attractive city in the world for 4 years in a row by the [Euromonitor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euromonitor) Global Cities Destination Index. However, by the number of tourists coming from other countries, it was only in 9th place.[^cite_note-204]

Tourism continued to recover in the Paris region in 2022, increasing to 44 million visitors, an increase of 95 percent over 2021, but still 13 percent lower than in 2019.[^cite_note-pro.visitparisregion.com-205]

[Greater Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Paris), comprising Paris and its three surrounding departments, received a record 38 million visitors in 2019, measured by hotel arrivals.[^cite_note-KeyFigures-206] These included 12.2 million French visitors. Of the foreign visitors, the greatest number came from the United States (2.6 million), the United Kingdom (1.2 million), Germany (981 thousand), and China (711 thousand).[^cite_note-KeyFigures-206]

In 2018, Paris was the second-busiest airline destination in the world, with 19.10 million visitors, behind Bangkok (22.78 million) but ahead of London (19.09 million).[^cite_note-207] In 2016, 393,008 workers in Greater Paris, or 12.4 percent of the total workforce, were engaged in tourism-related sectors such as hotels, catering, transport and leisure.[^cite_note-TIP2016-208]

Paris's top cultural attractions in 2022 were the [Louvre Museum](17546.md) (7.7 million visitors), the [Eiffel Tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eiffel_Tower) (5.8 million visitors), the [Musée d'Orsay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mus%C3%A9e_d'Orsay) (3.27 million visitors) and the [Centre Pompidou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centre_Pompidou) (3 million visitors).[^cite_note-pro.visitparisregion.com-205]

In 2019, Greater Paris had 2,056 hotels, including 94 five-star hotels, with a total of 121,646 rooms.[^cite_note-KeyFigures-206] In 2019, in addition to the hotels, Greater Paris had 60,000 homes registered with [Airbnb](32058290.md).[^cite_note-KeyFigures-206] Under French law, renters of these units must pay the Paris tourism tax. The company paid the city government 7.3 million euros in 2016.[^cite_note-209]

A minuscule fraction of foreign visitors suffer from [Paris syndrome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_syndrome), when their experiences do not meet expectations.[^cite_note-210]

## Culture

### Painting and sculpture

Main article: [Art in Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_in_Paris)



[Auguste Renoir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auguste_Renoir), *[Bal du moulin de la Galette](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bal_du_moulin_de_la_Galette)*, 1876, oil on canvas, 131 cm × 175 cm (52 in × 69 in), [Musée d'Orsay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mus%C3%A9e_d'Orsay)

For centuries, Paris has attracted artists from around the world. As a result, Paris has acquired a reputation as the "City of Art".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMontclos2003-211] Italian artists were a profound influence on the development of art in Paris in the 16th and 17th centuries, particularly in sculpture and reliefs. Painting and sculpture became the pride of the French monarchy, and the French royal family commissioned many Parisian artists to adorn their palaces during the [French Baroque and Classicism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Baroque_and_Classicism) era. Sculptors such as [Girardon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fran%C3%A7ois_Girardon), [Coysevox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antoine_Coysevox), and [Coustou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicolas_Coustou) acquired reputations as the finest artists in the royal court in 17th-century France. [Pierre Mignard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pierre_Mignard) became the first painter to King [Louis XIV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_XIV) during this period. In 1648, the *[Académie royale de peinture et de sculpture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acad%C3%A9mie_royale_de_peinture_et_de_sculpture)* (Royal Academy of Painting and Sculpture) was established to accommodate the dramatic interest in art in the capital.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMichelin2011-212]

Paris was in its artistic prime in the 19th century and early 20th century, when it had a colony of artists established in the city and in art schools associated with some of the finest painters of the times: [Henri de Toulouse-Lautrec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henri_de_Toulouse-Lautrec), [Édouard Manet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89douard_Manet), [Claude Monet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Claude_Monet), [Berthe Morisot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berthe_Morisot), [Paul Gauguin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Gauguin), [Pierre-Auguste Renoir](24546.md) and others. Paris was central to the development of [Romanticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanticism) in art, with painters such as [Géricault](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Th%C3%A9odore_G%C3%A9ricault).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMichelin2011-212] [Impressionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impressionism), [Art Nouveau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_Nouveau), [Symbolism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Symbolism_\(arts\)), [Fauvism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fauvism), [Cubism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cubism) and [Art Deco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_Deco) movements all evolved in Paris.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMichelin2011-212] In the late 19th century, many artists in the French provinces and worldwide flocked to Paris to exhibit their works in the numerous salons and expositions and make a name for themselves.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPerry199519-213] Artists such as [Pablo Picasso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pablo_Picasso), [Henri Matisse](60203.md), [Vincent van Gogh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vincent_van_Gogh), [Paul Cézanne](24472.md), [Jean Metzinger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean_Metzinger), [Albert Gleizes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albert_Gleizes), [Henri Rousseau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henri_Rousseau), [Marc Chagall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marc_Chagall), [Amedeo Modigliani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amedeo_Modigliani) became associated with Paris.

The most prestigious sculptors who made their reputation in Paris in the modern era are [Frédéric Auguste Bartholdi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fr%C3%A9d%C3%A9ric_Auguste_Bartholdi) ([Statue of Liberty](28617.md)), [Auguste Rodin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auguste_Rodin), [Camille Claudel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camille_Claudel), [Antoine Bourdelle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antoine_Bourdelle), [Paul Landowski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Landowski) ([*Christ the Redeemer*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christ_the_Redeemer_\(statue\)) in [Rio de Janeiro](25936.md)) and [Aristide Maillol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristide_Maillol). The [Golden Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belle_%C3%89poque) of the [School of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/School_of_Paris) ended between the two world wars.

### Museums

Main article: [List of museums in Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_museums_in_Paris)



[Musée d'Orsay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mus%C3%A9e_d'Orsay)



[Musée du quai Branly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mus%C3%A9e_du_Quai_Branly_%E2%80%93_Jacques_Chirac)

The [Louvre](17546.md) received 2.8 million visitors in 2021,[^cite_note-214] holding its position as the [world's most-visited museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_most-visited_museums). Its treasures include the *[Mona Lisa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mona_Lisa)*, the *[Venus de Milo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venus_de_Milo)* statue, and *[Liberty Leading the People](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberty_Leading_the_People)*. The second-most visited museum, with 1.5 million visitors in 2021, was the [Centre Georges Pompidou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centre_Georges_Pompidou), also known as Beaubourg, which houses the [Musée National d'Art Moderne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mus%C3%A9e_National_d'Art_Moderne). The [National Museum of Natural History](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Museum_of_Natural_History,_France) followed with 1.4 million visitors. It is famous for its dinosaur artefacts, mineral collections, and its Gallery of Evolution.[^cite_note-215]

The [Musée d'Orsay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mus%C3%A9e_d'Orsay), featuring 19th-century art and the [French Impressionists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Impressionists), had one million visitors. Paris hosts one of the largest science museums in Europe, the [Cité des sciences et de l'industrie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cit%C3%A9_des_sciences_et_de_l'industrie) (984,000 visitors in 2020), and one of the oldest, the [Musée des Arts et Métiers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mus%C3%A9e_des_Arts_et_M%C3%A9tiers) (opened in 1794). The other most-visited museums in 2021 were the [Fondation Louis Vuitton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fondation_Louis_Vuitton) (691,000), the [Musée du Quai Branly – Jacques Chirac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mus%C3%A9e_du_Quai_Branly_%E2%80%93_Jacques_Chirac), featuring indigenous art and cultures of Africa, Asia, Oceania, and the Americas. (616,000); the [Musée Carnavalet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mus%C3%A9e_Carnavalet) (History of Paris) (606,000), and the [Petit Palais](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petit_Palais), the art museum of the City of Paris (518,000).[^cite_note-216]

The [Musée de l'Orangerie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mus%C3%A9e_de_l'Orangerie), near the Louvre and the Orsay, also exhibits Impressionists and Post-Impressionists, including most of [Claude Monet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Claude_Monet)'s large *[Water Lilies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_Lilies_\(Monet_series\))* murals. The [Musée national du Moyen Âge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mus%C3%A9e_national_du_Moyen_%C3%82ge), or Cluny Museum, presents [Medieval art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medieval_art). The [Guimet Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guimet_Museum), or *Musée national des arts asiatiques*, has one of the largest collections of Asian art in Europe. There are also notable museums devoted to individual artists, including the [Musée Picasso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mus%C3%A9e_Picasso), the [Musée Rodin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mus%C3%A9e_Rodin), and the [Musée national Eugène Delacroix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mus%C3%A9e_national_Eug%C3%A8ne_Delacroix).

The military history of France is presented by displays at the [Musée de l'Armée](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mus%C3%A9e_de_l'Arm%C3%A9e) at [Les Invalides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Les_Invalides). In addition to the national museums, run by the [Ministry of Culture](3354559.md), the City of Paris operates 14 museums, including the [Carnavalet Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carnavalet_Museum) on the history of Paris, [Musée d'Art Moderne de la Ville de Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mus%C3%A9e_d'Art_Moderne_de_la_Ville_de_Paris), [Palais de Tokyo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palais_de_Tokyo), the [House of Victor Hugo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maison_de_Victor_Hugo), the [House of Balzac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maison_de_Balzac) and the [Catacombs of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catacombs_of_Paris).[^cite_note-217] There are also notable private museums. The Contemporary Art museum of the [Louis Vuitton Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_Vuitton_Foundation), designed by architect [Frank Gehry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frank_Gehry), opened in October 2014 in the [Bois de Boulogne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bois_de_Boulogne).

### Theatre

See also: [Theatre of France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theatre_of_France)



The [Opéra Bastille](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Op%C3%A9ra_Bastille)

The largest opera houses of Paris are the 19th-century Opéra Garnier (historical [Paris Opéra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Op%C3%A9ra_National_de_Paris)) and modern [Opéra Bastille](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Op%C3%A9ra_Bastille). The Opéra Garnier tends toward the more classic ballets and operas. The Paris Opéra provides a mixed repertoire of classic and modern.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELawrenceGondrand2010178-218] In the mid-19th century, there were three other active and competing opera houses: the Opéra-Comique (which still exists), [Théâtre-Italien](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Th%C3%A9%C3%A2tre-Italien) and [Théâtre Lyrique](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Th%C3%A9%C3%A2tre_Lyrique) (now the [Théâtre de la Ville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Th%C3%A9%C3%A2tre_de_la_Ville)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchumacher199660-219] [Philharmonie de Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philharmonie_de_Paris), the modern symphonic concert hall of Paris, opened in January 2015. Another musical landmark is the [Théâtre des Champs-Élysées](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Th%C3%A9%C3%A2tre_des_Champs-%C3%89lys%C3%A9es), where the first performances of Diaghilev's [Ballets Russes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ballets_Russes) took place in 1913.



The [Comédie Française](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Com%C3%A9die_Fran%C3%A7aise) (Salle Richelieu)

Theatre traditionally has occupied a large place in Parisian culture, and many of its most popular actors today are also stars of French television. The oldest and most famous Paris theatre is the [Comédie-Française](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Com%C3%A9die-Fran%C3%A7aise), founded in 1680. Run by the Government of France, it performs mostly French classics at the Salle Richelieu in the [Palais-Royal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palais-Royal).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFierro19961173-220] Other famous theatres include the [Odéon-Théâtre de l'Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Od%C3%A9on-Th%C3%A9%C3%A2tre_de_l'Europe), also a state institution and theatrical landmark; the Théâtre Mogador; and the [Théâtre de la Gaîté-Montparnasse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Th%C3%A9%C3%A2tre_de_la_Ga%C3%AEt%C3%A9-Montparnasse).[^cite_note-221]

The music hall and [cabaret](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cabaret) are famous Paris institutions. The *[Moulin Rouge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moulin_Rouge)* opened in 1889 and became the birthplace of the [Cancan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cancan) dance. It helped make famous the singers [Mistinguett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mistinguett) and [Édith Piaf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89dith_Piaf) and the painter [Toulouse-Lautrec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henri_de_Toulouse-Lautrec), who made posters for the venue. In 1911, the dance hall [Olympia Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olympia_Paris) invented the grand staircase as a setting for its shows, competing with its great rival, the *[Folies Bergère](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Folies_Berg%C3%A8re)*.

The [Casino de Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casino_de_Paris) presented many famous French singers, including [Mistinguett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mistinguett), [Maurice Chevalier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maurice_Chevalier) and [Tino Rossi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tino_Rossi). Other famous Paris music halls include *[Le Lido](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Le_Lido)*, on the Champs-Élysées, opened in 1946; and the [Crazy Horse Saloon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crazy_Horse_\(cabaret\)), featuring strip-tease, dance, and magic, opened in 1951. A half dozen music halls exist today in Paris, attended mostly by visitors to the city.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFierro19961005–1006-222]

### Literature

Main article: [Writers in Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Writers_in_Paris)



[Victor Hugo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victor_Hugo)

The first book printed in France, *Epistolae* ("Letters"), by [Gasparinus de Bergamo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gasparinus_de_Bergamo), was published in Paris in 1470 by the press established by [Johann Heynlin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johann_Heynlin). Paris has since been the centre of the French publishing industry, the home of some of the world's best-known writers and poets, and the setting for many classic works of French literature. Paris did not become the acknowledged capital of French literature until the 17th century, with authors such as [Boileau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicolas_Boileau), [Corneille](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pierre_Corneille), [La Fontaine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Fontaine), [Molière](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moli%C3%A8re), [Racine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean_Racine), [Charles Perrault](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Perrault),[^cite_note-223] several coming from the provinces, as well as the foundation of the [Académie française](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acad%C3%A9mie_fran%C3%A7aise).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFierro1996488-224] In the 18th century, the literary life of Paris revolved around the cafés and salons; it was dominated by [Voltaire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voltaire), [Jean-Jacques Rousseau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Jacques_Rousseau), [Pierre de Marivaux](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pierre_de_Marivaux), and [Pierre Beaumarchais](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pierre_Beaumarchais).

During the 19th century, Paris was the home and subject for some of France's greatest writers, including [Charles Baudelaire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Baudelaire), [Stéphane Mallarmé](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St%C3%A9phane_Mallarm%C3%A9), [Mérimée](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C3%A9rim%C3%A9e), [Alfred de Musset](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfred_de_Musset), [Marcel Proust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marcel_Proust), [Émile Zola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89mile_Zola), [Alexandre Dumas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandre_Dumas), [Gustave Flaubert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gustave_Flaubert), [Guy de Maupassant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guy_de_Maupassant) and [Honoré de Balzac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Honor%C3%A9_de_Balzac). Victor Hugo's *[The Hunchback of Notre-Dame](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Hunchback_of_Notre-Dame)* inspired the renovation of its setting, the [Notre-Dame de Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Notre-Dame_de_Paris).[^cite_note-225] Another of Hugo's works, *[Les Misérables](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Les_Mis%C3%A9rables)*, described the social change and political turmoil in Paris in the early 1830s.[^cite_note-226] One of the most popular of all French writers, [Jules Verne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jules_Verne), worked at the Theatre Lyrique and the Paris stock exchange while he did research for his stories at the National Library.[^cite_note-227]

In the 20th century, the Paris literary community was dominated by figures such as [Colette](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colette), [André Gide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andr%C3%A9_Gide), [François Mauriac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fran%C3%A7ois_Mauriac), [André Malraux](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andr%C3%A9_Malraux), [Albert Camus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albert_Camus), and, after World War II, by [Simone de Beauvoir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simone_de_Beauvoir) and [Jean-Paul Sartre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Paul_Sartre). Between the wars, it was the home of many important expatriate writers, including [Ernest Hemingway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernest_Hemingway), [Samuel Beckett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_Beckett), [Miguel Ángel Asturias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miguel_%C3%81ngel_Asturias), [Alejo Carpentier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alejo_Carpentier), and [Arturo Uslar Pietri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arturo_Uslar_Pietri). The winner of the 2014 [Nobel Prize in Literature](23385442.md), [Patrick Modiano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patrick_Modiano), based most of his literary work on the depiction of the city during World War II and the 1960s–1970s.[^cite_note-228]

In the 1970s, 80 percent of French-language publishing houses were found in Paris.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFierro1996840-229] It is also a city of small bookstores. There are about 150 bookstores in the 5th arrondissement alone, plus another 250 book stalls along the Seine. Small Paris bookstores are protected against competition from discount booksellers by French law; books, even e-books, cannot be discounted more than five percent below their publisher's cover price.[^cite_note-230]

### Music

Main articles: [Music in Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_in_Paris) and [History of music in Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_music_in_Paris)



[Olympia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olympia_\(Paris\)) music hall

In the late 12th century, a school of [polyphony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polyphony) was established at Notre-Dame. Among the [Trouvères](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trouv%C3%A8res) of northern France, a group of Parisian aristocrats became known for their poetry and songs. [Troubadours](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Troubadours), from the south of France, were also popular. During the reign of [François I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francis_I_of_France), in the [Renaissance era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance_music), the [lute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lute) became popular in the French court. The French royal family and courtiers "disported themselves in masques, ballets, allegorical dances, recitals, and opera and comedy", and a national musical printing house was established.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMichelin2011-212] In the [Baroque-era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baroque_music), noted composers included [Jean-Baptiste Lully](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Baptiste_Lully), [Jean-Philippe Rameau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Philippe_Rameau), and [François Couperin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fran%C3%A7ois_Couperin).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMichelin2011-212] The [*Conservatoire de Musique de Paris*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservatoire_de_Paris) was founded in 1795.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDamschroederWilliams1990157-231] By 1870, Paris had become an important centre for symphony, ballet, and operatic music.

[Romantic-era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romantic_music) composers include [Hector Berlioz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hector_Berlioz), [Charles Gounod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Gounod), [Camille Saint-Saëns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camille_Saint-Sa%C3%ABns), [Léo Delibes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L%C3%A9o_Delibes) and [Jules Massenet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jules_Massenet), among others.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMichelin2011-212] [Georges Bizet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georges_Bizet)'s *[Carmen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carmen)* premiered in 1875 and has become one of the most popular operas in the classical [canon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_canon).[^cite_note-McClary-232][^cite_note-233] Among the [Impressionist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impressionism_in_music) composers who created new works for piano, orchestra, opera, chamber music and other musical forms, stand in particular [Claude Debussy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Claude_Debussy), [Erik Satie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erik_Satie) and [Maurice Ravel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maurice_Ravel). Several foreign-born composers, such as [Frédéric Chopin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fr%C3%A9d%C3%A9ric_Chopin), [Franz Liszt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franz_Liszt), [Jacques Offenbach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacques_Offenbach), [Niccolò Paganini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niccol%C3%B2_Paganini), and [Igor Stravinsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Igor_Stravinsky), established themselves or made significant contributions both with their works and their influence in Paris.

[Bal-musette](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bal-musette) is a style of French music and dance that first became popular in Paris in the 1870s; by 1880, Paris had some 150 dance halls.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDregni200419-234] Patrons danced the [bourrée](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bourr%C3%A9e) to the accompaniment of the [cabrette](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cabrette) (a [bellows](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bellows)-blown [bagpipe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bagpipe) locally called a "musette") and the vielle à roue ([hurdy-gurdy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurdy-gurdy)) in the cafés and bars of the city. Parisian and Italian musicians who played the [accordion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Accordion) adopted the style and established themselves in Auvergnat bars,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDregni200832-235] and Paris became a major centre for [jazz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jazz) and still attracts jazz musicians from all around the world to its clubs and cafés.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMroue2006260-236] Paris is the spiritual home of [gypsy jazz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gypsy_jazz), and many of the Parisian jazzmen who developed in the first half of the 20th century began by playing Bal-musette in the city.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDregni200832-235] [Django Reinhardt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Django_Reinhardt) rose to fame in Paris and performed with violinist [Stéphane Grappelli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St%C3%A9phane_Grappelli) and their [Quintette du Hot Club de France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quintette_du_Hot_Club_de_France) in the 1930s and 1940s.[^cite_note-Gjazz-237]



The [Moulin Rouge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moulin_Rouge) has hosted many singers including Parisian [Édith Piaf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89dith_Piaf)

Immediately after the war, the [Saint-Germain-des-Prés](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint-Germain-des-Pr%C3%A9s) quarter and the nearby Saint-Michel quarter became home to many small jazz clubs, including the Caveau des Lorientais, the Club Saint-Germain, the Rose Rouge, the Vieux-Colombier, and the most famous, [Le Tabou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Le_Tabou). They introduced Parisians to the music of [Claude Luter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Claude_Luter), [Boris Vian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boris_Vian), [Sydney Bechet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sydney_Bechet), [Mezz Mezzrow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mezz_Mezzrow), and [Henri Salvador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henri_Salvador). Most of the clubs closed by the early 1960s, as musical tastes shifted toward rock and roll.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBezbakh2004872-238]

Some of the finest [manouche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manouche) musicians in the world are found here playing the cafés of the city at night.[^cite_note-Gjazz-237] Some of the more notable jazz venues include the New Morning, Le Sunset, La Chope des Puces and Bouquet du Nord.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMroue2006260-236][^cite_note-Gjazz-237] Several yearly festivals take place in Paris, including the [Paris Jazz Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_Jazz_Festival) and the rock festival [Rock en Seine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rock_en_Seine).[^cite_note-239] The [Orchestre de Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orchestre_de_Paris) was established in 1967.[^cite_note-andante-240] [Édith Piaf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89dith_Piaf) is widely regarded as France's national [chanteuse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chanson) and one of France's greatest international stars.[^cite_note-yahoomusicbio-241]

Paris has a big [hip hop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_hip_hop) scene. This music became popular during the 1980s.[^cite_note-242]

### Cinema

See also: [List of films set in Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_films_set_in_Paris)



A poster for the [Lumière brothers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auguste_and_Louis_Lumi%C3%A8re) 1895 film *[L'Arroseur Arrosé](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L'Arroseur_Arros%C3%A9)*, the earliest comedy, and the first film to portray a fictional story

The movie industry was born in Paris when [Auguste and Louis Lumière](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auguste_and_Louis_Lumi%C3%A8re) projected the first motion picture for a paying audience at the Grand Café on 28 December 1895.[^cite_note-243] Many of Paris's concert/dance halls were transformed into cinemas when the media became popular beginning in the 1930s. Paris's largest cinema room today is in the [Grand Rex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Rex) theatre with 2,700 seats.[^cite_note-244] Big multiplex cinemas have been built since the 1990s. UGC Ciné Cité Les Halles, with 27 screens, MK2 Bibliothèque with 20 screens, and UGC Ciné Cité Bercy with 18 screens are among the largest.[^cite_note-245]

Parisians tend to share the same movie-going trends as many of the world's global cities, with cinemas primarily dominated by Hollywood-generated film entertainment. [French cinema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_France) comes a close second, with major directors (*réalisateurs*) such as [Claude Lelouch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Claude_Lelouch), [Jean-Luc Godard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Luc_Godard), and [Luc Besson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luc_Besson), and the more slapstick/popular genre with director [Claude Zidi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Claude_Zidi) as an example.[^cite_note-246]

### Restaurants and cuisine

See also: [French cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_cuisine)



Le Zimmer, on the [Place du Châtelet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Place_du_Ch%C3%A2telet)

Since the late 18th century, Paris has been famous for its restaurants and *[haute cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haute_cuisine)*, food meticulously prepared and artfully presented. La Taverne Anglaise, opened in 1786 in the arcades of the [Palais-Royal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palais-Royal) by [Antoine Beauvilliers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antoine_Beauvilliers); it became a model for future luxury Paris restaurants. The restaurant [Le Grand Véfour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Le_Grand_V%C3%A9four) in the Palais-Royal dates from the same period.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFierro19961136–1138-247] The famous Paris restaurants of the 19th century, including the Café de Paris, the [Rocher de Cancale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Au_Rocher_de_Cancale), the [Café Anglais](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caf%C3%A9_Anglais), [Maison Dorée](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maison_dor%C3%A9e_\(Paris\)) and the Café Riche, were mostly located near the theatres on the [Boulevard des Italiens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boulevard_des_Italiens). Several of the best-known restaurants in Paris today appeared during the *[Belle Époque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belle_%C3%89poque)*, including [Maxim's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maxim's_Paris) on Rue Royale, [Ledoyen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ledoyen) in the gardens of the [Champs-Élysées](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Champs-%C3%89lys%C3%A9es), and the [Tour d'Argent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tour_d'Argent) on the Quai de la Tournelle.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFierro19961137-248]

Today, owing to Paris's cosmopolitan population, every French regional cuisine and almost every national cuisine in the world can be found there; the city has more than 9,000 restaurants.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDominé2014-249] The [Michelin Guide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michelin_Guide) has been a standard guide to French restaurants since 1900, awarding its highest award, three stars, to the best restaurants in France. In 2018, of the 27 Michelin three-star restaurants in France, ten are located in Paris. These include both restaurants that serve classical French cuisine, such as [L'Ambroisie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L'Ambroisie), and those that serve non-traditional menus, such as [L'Astrance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L'Astrance), which combines French and Asian cuisines. Several of France's most famous chefs, including [Pierre Gagnaire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pierre_Gagnaire), [Alain Ducasse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alain_Ducasse), [Yannick Alléno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yannick_All%C3%A9no), and [Alain Passard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alain_Passard), have three-star restaurants in Paris.[^cite_note-250][^cite_note-251]



[Les Deux Magots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Les_Deux_Magots) café on Boulevard Saint-Germain

Paris has several other kinds of traditional eating places. The [café](370815.md) arrived in Paris in the 17th century, and by the 18th century, Parisian cafés were centres of the city's political and cultural life. The [Café Procope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caf%C3%A9_Procope) on the Left Bank dates from this period. In the 20th century, the cafés of the Left Bank, especially [Café de la Rotonde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caf%C3%A9_de_la_Rotonde) and [Le Dôme Café](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Le_D%C3%B4me_Caf%C3%A9) in Montparnasse and [Café de Flore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caf%C3%A9_de_Flore) and [Les Deux Magots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Les_Deux_Magots) on Boulevard Saint Germain, all still in business, were important meeting places for painters, writers and philosophers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFierro19961137-248]

A [bistro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bistro) is a type of eating place loosely defined as a neighbourhood restaurant with a modest decor and prices and a regular clientele, and a congenial atmosphere. Real bistros are increasingly rare in Paris, due to rising costs, competition, and different eating habits of Parisian diners.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFierro1996715-252] A [brasserie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brasserie) originally was a tavern located next to a brewery, which served beer and food at any hour. Beginning with the [Paris Exposition of 1867](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_Exposition_of_1867), it became a popular kind of restaurant which featured beer and other beverages served by young women in the national costume associated with the beverage. Now, brasseries, like cafés, serve food and drinks throughout the day.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFierro1996773-253]

### Fashion

Main article: [Fashion in Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fashion_in_Paris)



[Magdalena Frackowiak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magdalena_Frackowiak) at [Paris Fashion Week](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_Fashion_Week), Autumn 2011

Since the 19th century, Paris has been an international [fashion capital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fashion_capital), particularly in the domain of [haute couture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haute_couture) (clothing hand-made to order for private clients).[^cite_note-254] It is home to some of the largest fashion houses in the world, including [Dior](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Dior_S.A.) and [Chanel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chanel), as well as many other well-known and more contemporary fashion designers, such as [Karl Lagerfeld](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karl_Lagerfeld), [Jean-Paul Gaultier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Paul_Gaultier), [Yves Saint Laurent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yves_Saint_Laurent_\(designer\)), [Givenchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Givenchy), and [Christian Lacroix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Lacroix). [Paris Fashion Week](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_Fashion_Week), held in January and July in the [Carrousel du Louvre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carrousel_du_Louvre) among other renowned city locations, is one of the top four events on the international fashion calendar.[^cite_note-255][^cite_note-256]

Paris is also the home of the world's largest [cosmetics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cosmetics) company, [L'Oréal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L'Or%C3%A9al), as well as three of the top five global makers of luxury fashion accessories: [Louis Vuitton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_Vuitton), [Hermés](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herm%C3%A9s), and [Cartier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cartier_\(jeweler\)).[^cite_note-257] Most of the major fashion designers have their showrooms along the [Avenue Montaigne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avenue_Montaigne), between the [Champs-Élysées](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Champs-%C3%89lys%C3%A9es) and the Seine.

### Photography

The inventor [Nicéphore Niépce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nic%C3%A9phore_Ni%C3%A9pce) produced the first permanent photograph on a polished pewter plate in Paris in 1825. In 1839, [Louis Daguerre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_Daguerre) patented the [Daguerreotype](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daguerreotype), which became the most common form of photography until the 1860s.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMichelin2011-212] The work of [Étienne-Jules Marey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89tienne-Jules_Marey) in the 1880s contributed considerably to the development of modern photography. Photography came to occupy a central role in Parisian Surrealist activity, in the works of [Man Ray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Man_Ray) and [Maurice Tabard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maurice_Tabard).[^cite_note-258][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHazan2011362-259]

Numerous photographers achieved renown for their photography of Paris, including [Eugène Atget](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eug%C3%A8ne_Atget), noted for his depictions of street scenes; [Robert Doisneau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Doisneau), noted for his playful pictures of people and market scenes (among which *Le baiser de l'hôtel de ville* has become iconic of the romantic vision of Paris); [Marcel Bovis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marcel_Bovis), noted for his night scenes; [Jacques-Henri Lartigue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacques-Henri_Lartigue); and [Henri Cartier-Bresson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henri_Cartier-Bresson).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMichelin2011-212] [Poster art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poster_art) also became an important art form in Paris in the late nineteenth century, through the work of [Henri de Toulouse-Lautrec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henri_de_Toulouse-Lautrec), [Jules Chéret](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jules_Ch%C3%A9ret), [Eugène Grasset](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eug%C3%A8ne_Grasset), [Adolphe Willette](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adolphe_Willette), [Pierre Bonnard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pierre_Bonnard), [Georges de Feure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georges_de_Feure), [Henri-Gabriel Ibels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henri-Gabriel_Ibels), [Paul Gavarni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Gavarni) and [Alphonse Mucha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alphonse_Mucha).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMichelin2011-212]

### Media



Founded in 1826, *[Le Figaro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Le_Figaro)* is still considered a [newspaper of record](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newspaper_of_record).

[^cite_note-260]



Agence France-Presse Headquarters in Paris

Paris and its close suburbs are home to numerous newspapers, magazines and publications including *[Le Monde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Le_Monde)*, *[Le Figaro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Le_Figaro)*, *[Libération](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lib%C3%A9ration)*, *[Le Nouvel Observateur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Le_Nouvel_Observateur)*, *[Le Canard enchaîné](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Le_Canard_encha%C3%AEn%C3%A9)*, *[La Croix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Croix_\(newspaper\))*, *[Le Parisien](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Le_Parisien)* (in [Saint-Ouen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint-Ouen,_Seine-Saint-Denis)), *[Les Échos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Les_%C3%89chos_\(France\))*, *[Paris Match](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_Match)* ([Neuilly-sur-Seine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neuilly-sur-Seine)), *Réseaux & Télécoms*, [Reuters](18998750.md) France, *[l'Équipe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L'%C3%89quipe)* ([Boulogne-Billancourt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boulogne-Billancourt)) and *L'Officiel des Spectacles*.[^cite_note-261] France's two most prestigious newspapers, *Le Monde* and *Le Figaro*, are the centrepieces of the Parisian publishing industry.[^cite_note-262] [Agence France-Presse](471981.md) is France's oldest continually operating news agency and is headquartered in Paris.[^cite_note-263] [France 24](1263244.md) is a television news channel owned and operated by the French government.[^cite_note-264] France Diplomatie, owned and operated by the [Ministry of Foreign and European Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Foreign_and_European_Affairs_\(France\)), pertains solely to diplomatic news and occurrences.[^cite_note-265]

The most-viewed network in France, [TF1](571365.md), is in nearby [Boulogne-Billancourt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boulogne-Billancourt). [France 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/France_2), [France 3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/France_3), [Canal+](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canal+_\(French_TV_channel\)), [France 5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/France_5), [M6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M6_\(TV_channel\)) ([Neuilly-sur-Seine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neuilly-sur-Seine)), [Arte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arte), [D8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D8_\(TV_channel\)), [W9](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/W9_\(TV_channel\)), [NT1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NT1_\(TV_channel\)), [NRJ 12](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NRJ_12), [La Chaîne parlementaire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Cha%C3%AEne_parlementaire), [France 4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/France_4), [BFM TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BFM_TV), and [Gulli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulli) are other stations located in and around the capital.[^cite_note-266] [Radio France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_France), France's public radio broadcaster, and its various channels, is headquartered in Paris's [16th arrondissement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/16th_arrondissement_of_Paris). [Radio France Internationale](3866201.md), another public broadcaster, is also based in the city.[^cite_note-267] Paris also holds the headquarters of the [La Poste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Poste_\(France\)), France's national postal carrier.[^cite_note-268]

### Holidays and festivals

[Bastille Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bastille_Day), a celebration of the storming of the Bastille in 1789, the biggest festival in the city, is a military parade taking place every year on 14 July on the [Champs-Élysées](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Champs-%C3%89lys%C3%A9es), from the [Arc de Triomphe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arc_de_Triomphe) to [Place de la Concorde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Place_de_la_Concorde). It includes a [flypast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flypast) over the Champs Élysées by the [Patrouille de France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patrouille_de_France), a parade of military units and equipment, and a display of fireworks in the evening, the most spectacular being the one at the Eiffel Tower.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlackmoreMcConnachie2004204-269]

Other yearly festivals include [Paris-Plages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris-Plages), a festive summertime event when the Right Bank of the Seine is converted into a temporary beach;[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlackmoreMcConnachie2004204-269] [Journées du Patrimoine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Heritage_Days), [Fête de la Musique](745073.md), Techno Parade, [Nuit Blanche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuit_Blanche), Cinéma au clair de lune, Printemps des rues, Festival d'automne, and Fête des jardins. The [Carnaval de Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_Carnival), one of the oldest festivals in Paris, dates back to the Middle Ages.

### Libraries

Main article: [Libraries in Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libraries_in_Paris)



[Sainte-Geneviève Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sainte-Genevi%C3%A8ve_Library)

The *[Bibliothèque nationale de France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biblioth%C3%A8que_nationale_de_France)* (BnF) operates public libraries in Paris, among them the François Mitterrand Library, Richelieu Library, Louvois, Opéra Library, and [Arsenal Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biblioth%C3%A8que_de_l'Arsenal).[^cite_note-270]

The [Bibliothèque Forney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biblioth%C3%A8que_Forney), in the Marais district, is dedicated to the decorative arts; the Arsenal Library occupies a former military building, and has a large collection on French literature; and the [Bibliothèque historique de la ville de Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biblioth%C3%A8que_historique_de_la_ville_de_Paris), also in Le Marais, contains the Paris historical research service. The [Sainte-Geneviève Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sainte-Genevi%C3%A8ve_Library), designed by [Henri Labrouste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henri_Labrouste) and built in the mid-1800s, contains a rare book and manuscript division.[^cite_note-Woodward2006-271] [Bibliothèque Mazarine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biblioth%C3%A8que_Mazarine) is the oldest public library in France. The [Médiathèque Musicale Mahler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C3%A9diath%C3%A8que_Musicale_Mahler) opened in 1986 and contains collections related to music. The François Mitterrand Library (nicknamed *Très Grande Bibliothèque*) was completed in 1994 to a design of [Dominique Perrault](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominique_Perrault) and contains four glass towers.[^cite_note-Woodward2006-271]

There are several academic libraries and archives in Paris. The [Sorbonne Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biblioth%C3%A8que_de_la_Sorbonne) is the largest university library in Paris. In addition to the [Sorbonne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sorbonne_\(building\)) location, there are branches in Malesherbes, Clignancourt-Championnet, Michelet-[Institut d'Art et d'Archéologie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institut_d'Art_et_d'Arch%C3%A9ologie), Serpente-Maison de la Recherche, and Institut des Etudes Ibériques.[^cite_note-272] Other academic libraries include Interuniversity Pharmaceutical Library, Leonardo da Vinci University Library, Paris School of Mines Library, and the René Descartes University Library.[^cite_note-nd.edu-273]

## Sports

See also: [Football in Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Football_in_Paris)



[Parc des Princes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parc_des_Princes), the stadium of Paris Saint-Germain

Paris's major sports clubs are the [association football](10568.md) club [Paris Saint-Germain F.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_Saint-Germain_F.C.) (PSG)[^cite_note-274] and the [rugby union](25405.md) clubs [Stade Français](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stade_Fran%C3%A7ais) and [Racing 92](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racing_92).[^cite_note-275] The 80,000-seat [Stade de France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stade_de_France), built for the [1998 FIFA World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1998_FIFA_World_Cup), is located just north of Paris in the commune of [Saint-Denis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint-Denis,_Seine-Saint-Denis).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELawrenceGondrand2010300–301-276] It is used for football, rugby union, and track and field athletics. It hosts the [France national football team](250197.md) for friendlies and major tournaments qualifiers, the [French national rugby team](955656.md)'s home matches of the [Six Nations Championship](26547.md), and several important matches of the Stade Français rugby team.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELawrenceGondrand2010300–301-276] The city and closer suburbs have several other professional and amateur football clubs, including [Paris FC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_FC) and [Red Star FC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Star_FC).[^cite_note-277][^cite_note-278]

[Rugby league](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rugby_league) is not as popular in Paris as in other areas of France. Despite this, the capital has hosted several major events in the sport's history since the country's first game, an exhibition match between the [Australia national team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australia_national_rugby_league_team) and a [British League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rugby_Football_League) Select XIII, in December 1933. The [Rugby League World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rugby_League_World_Cup) was first held in [1954](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1954_Rugby_League_World_Cup) with the [Parc des Princes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parc_des_Princes#Second_stadium_\(1932%E2%80%931972\)) hosting the opening match and the [final](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1954_Rugby_League_World_Cup_final) in which [France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/France_national_rugby_league_team) finished as runners-up to [Great Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Britain_national_rugby_league_team). The newly rebuilt [Parc des Princes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parc_des_Princes) was later used for a group game of the [1972 Rugby League World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1972_Rugby_League_World_Cup). A [rugby league department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_Saint-Germain_Rugby_League) of PSG was created to participate in the [Super League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_League).[^cite_note-279] In 2006, [Catalans Dragons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalans_Dragons) became the first French side to enter the [British rugby league system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_rugby_league_system) following PSG's withdrawal.[^cite_note-280]



The 2010 Tour de France, [Champs Élysées](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Champs_%C3%89lys%C3%A9es)

Paris was the first city to host the [Summer Olympics](27566.md) twice and the second to host three times, hosting in [1900](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1900_Summer_Olympics), [1924](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1924_Summer_Olympics) and [2024](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Summer_Olympics).[^cite_note-281] The city also hosted the finals of the [1938 FIFA World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1938_FIFA_World_Cup_final), [1998 FIFA World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1998_FIFA_World_Cup_final), [2007 Rugby World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007_Rugby_World_Cup_Final) and [2023 Rugby World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Rugby_World_Cup_final), at the Stade de France. Paris hosted the finals of the [1960](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1960_European_Nations'_Cup_final), [1984](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UEFA_Euro_1984_final) and [2016](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UEFA_Euro_2016_final) UEFA European Championships.[^cite_note-282] Paris held six [UEFA Champions League](44220.md) finals, including the [2022](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_UEFA_Champions_League_Final) edition.[^cite_note-283]

The final stage of the [Tour de France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tour_de_France) finishes in Paris; since 1975, the race has [finished on the Champs-Elysées](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Champs-%C3%89lys%C3%A9es_stage_in_the_Tour_de_France).[^cite_note-284] Two annual [tennis](29773.md) tournaments are held in Paris: the [French Open](147724.md), held every year on the [red clay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_clay) of the [Stade Roland Garros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stade_Roland_Garros),[^cite_note-285] one of the four [Grand Slam](197638.md) events of the world professional tennis tour, and the [Paris Masters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_Masters), held in the [Bercy Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AccorHotels_Arena).[^cite_note-286]

[Basketball](3921.md) teams [Levallois SCB](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levallois_Sporting_Club_Basket) and [Paris Basket Racing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_Basket_Racing) merged in 2007 to create club [Metropolitans 92](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitans_92), which plays some of its games at the [Stade Pierre de Coubertin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stade_Pierre_de_Coubertin_\(Paris\)).[^cite_note-287] Paris also features [Paris Basketball](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_Basketball) in Basketball,[^cite_note-288] [Paris Saint-Germain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_Saint-Germain_Handball) in [handball](13730.md)[^cite_note-289] and [Paris Musketeers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_Musketeers) in [American football](18951490.md).[^cite_note-290]

## Infrastructure

### Transport

Main article: [Transport in Paris](3410448.md)



The [Gare du Nord](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gare_du_Nord) railway station is the busiest in Europe.

Paris is a major rail, highway, and air transport hub. The [Île-de-France Mobilités](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%8Ele-de-France_Mobilit%C3%A9s) (IDFM) oversees the transit network in the region.[^cite_note-stif-291] The syndicate coordinates public transport. The [RATP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RATP_Group) operates 347 [bus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bus_\(RATP\)) lines, the [Métro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_M%C3%A9tro), eight tramway lines, and sections of the RER. The [SNCF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SNCF) operates suburban rails, one tramway line, and other sections of the RER. The [Optile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Optile) consortium of private operators manages 1,176 bus lines.[^cite_note-292]

Paris has one of the most [sustainable](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sustainable_transport) transportation systems in the world[^cite_note-ICLEI-293] and is one of only two cities that received the [Sustainable Transport Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sustainable_Transport_Award) twice, in 2008 and 2023.[^cite_note-294] In 2022–2023, 53.3% of trips in Paris were made on foot, 30% on public transport, 11.2% on bicycles and 4.3% on cars.[^cite_note-295][^cite_note-296] Paris has banned the most polluting automobiles from key districts[^cite_note-297][^cite_note-298] and is doubling bike lanes. The concept of the [15-minute city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/15-minute_city) was created by [Carlos Moreno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlos_Moreno_\(urbanist\)), a professor from Paris, and began to be implemented by its mayor [Anne Hidalgo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anne_Hidalgo).[^cite_note-299]

#### Walking

Paris is a highly [walkable](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walkable) city and tries to increase its mode share beyond[^cite_note-300][^cite_note-301] 53% of all trips in 2024.[^cite_note-302] The number of trips made by foot in the Grand Paris metropolis increased by 50% from 2000 to 2018.[^cite_note-303]

#### Railways

See also: [List of Paris railway stations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Paris_railway_stations)

Main articles: [Paris Métro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_M%C3%A9tro), [Réseau Express Régional](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R%C3%A9seau_Express_R%C3%A9gional), [Transilien](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transilien), and [Tramways in Île-de-France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tramways_in_%C3%8Ele-de-France)



The [Paris Métro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_M%C3%A9tro) is the busiest subway network in the European Union.

A central hub of the national rail network, Paris's six major railway stations ([Gare du Nord](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gare_du_Nord), [Gare de l'Est](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gare_de_l'Est), [Gare de Lyon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gare_de_Lyon), [Gare d'Austerlitz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gare_d'Austerlitz), [Gare Montparnasse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gare_Montparnasse), [Gare Saint-Lazare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gare_Saint-Lazare)) and a minor one ([Gare de Bercy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gare_de_Bercy)) are connected to three networks: [high-speed rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High-speed_rail) lines ([TGV inOui](57212.md), [TGV Lyria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TGV_Lyria), [TGV Ouigo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ouigo), [Eurostar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurostar), [Intercity Express](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intercity_Express), [Frecciarossa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frecciarossa)), normal speed trains ([Intercités](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intercit%C3%A9s), [Ouigo Train Classique](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ouigo), [Intercités de nuit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intercit%C3%A9s_de_nuit), [European Sleeper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Sleeper), [TER](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transport_express_r%C3%A9gional)), and the suburban rails ([Transilien](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transilien)). The Transilien is the [commuter rail](6286.md) network serving [Paris region](1716427.md), through 9 lines, 392 stations and 1,294 km (804.1 mi) of rails.

Since the inauguration of its first line in 1900, Paris's Métro network has grown to become the city's most widely used local transport system. In 2015, it carried about 5.23 million passengers daily.[^cite_note-304] There are 16 lines, 321 stations (405 stops) and 245.6 km (152.6 mi) of rails. Superimposed on this is a [regional express network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R%C3%A9seau_Express_R%C3%A9gional), the RER, whose five lines, 257 stops, and 602 km (374 mi) of rails connect Paris to more distant parts of the urban area. With over 1.4 million passengers per day, [RER A](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RER_A) is the busiest metro line in Europe. The [Paris region](1716427.md) is served by [a light rail network, the tramway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tramways_in_%C3%8Ele-de-France). Opened in 1992, fifteen lines are now operational, spanning 196.6 kilometres (122.2 mi) with 283 stations.

#### Air



In 2023, [Charles de Gaulle Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_de_Gaulle_Airport) was the third-busiest airport in Europe and the eleventh-busiest airport in the world.

[^cite_note-305]

Paris is served by three commercial international airports: [Charles de Gaulle Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_de_Gaulle_Airport), the [third-busiest airport in Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_the_busiest_airports_in_Europe) as of 2018,[^cite_note-306][^cite_note-307] [Orly Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orly_Airport) and [Beauvais–Tillé Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beauvais%E2%80%93Till%C3%A9_Airport). In 2019, these three airports recorded traffic of 112 million passengers,[^cite_note-308] making them the [5th busiest airport system in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World's_busiest_city_airport_systems_by_passenger_traffic). There is also one [general aviation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_aviation) airport, [Paris–Le Bourget Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris%E2%80%93Le_Bourget_Airport), historically the oldest Parisian airport and closest to the city centre, which is now used only for private business flights and air shows. Charles de Gaulle Airport, located on the edge of the northern suburbs of Paris, opened to commercial traffic in 1974 and became the busiest Parisian airport in 1993.[^cite_note-309] In 2023, it was the [4th busiest airport in the world by international traffic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_busiest_airports_by_international_passenger_traffic) and it is the hub for the nation's [flag carrier](465816.md), [Air France](14998012.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELawrenceGondrand2010278–283-310] Beauvais-Tillé Airport, located 69 km (43 mi) north of Paris's city centre, is used by charter airlines and [low-cost carriers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Low-cost_carriers).

#### Motorways



The [Boulevard Périphérique](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boulevard_P%C3%A9riph%C3%A9rique)

Paris is the most important hub of France's [motorway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motorway) network, and is surrounded by three orbital freeways: the [Périphérique](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P%C3%A9riph%C3%A9rique_\(Paris\)),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELawrenceGondrand201069-96] which follows the approximate path of 19th-century fortifications around Paris, the [A86](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A86_autoroute) motorway in the inner suburbs, and the [Francilienne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francilienne) motorway in the outer suburbs. Paris has an extensive road network with over 2,000 km (1,243 mi) of highways and motorways.

#### Waterways

The Paris region is the most active water transport area in France. Most of the cargo is handled by the [Ports of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autonomous_Port_of_Paris), in facilities located around Paris. The rivers [Loire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loire), [Rhine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhine), [Rhône](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rh%C3%B4ne), [Meuse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meuse), and [Scheldt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scheldt) can be reached by canals connecting with the Seine, which include the [Canal Saint-Martin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canal_Saint-Martin), [Canal Saint-Denis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canal_Saint-Denis), and the Canal de l'Ourcq.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJefferson2009114-311]

#### Cycling



[Vélib'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V%C3%A9lib') at the [Place de la Bastille](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Place_de_la_Bastille)

As of 2025 there were 1,565 km (972 mi) of [cycle paths and routes in Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cycling_in_Paris). These include *piste cyclable*, bike lanes separated from other traffic by physical barriers, and *bande cyclable*, a bicycle lane denoted by a painted path on the road.[^cite_note-312] Some 29 km (18 mi) of specially marked bus lanes are free for use by cyclists, with a protective barrier against encroachments from vehicles.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHart2004355-313] Cyclists have the right to ride in both directions on certain one-way streets. Paris has a [bike sharing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Community_bicycle_program) system called [Vélib'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V%C3%A9lib') with more than 20,000 public bicycles distributed at 1,800 parking stations.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERand2010165-314]

### Electricity

See also: [Electricity sector in France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electricity_sector_in_France)

In 2012, around 50% of electricity generated in the [Île-de-France](1716427.md) came from cogeneration energy plants. Other energy sources included thermal power (35%), waste incineration (9% – with cogeneration plants, these provide the city with heat as well), methane gas (5%), hydraulics (1%), and solar power (0.1%).[^cite_note-prod_electrique_IDF-315] A quarter of the city's [district heating](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_heating) is to come from a plant in [Saint-Ouen-sur-Seine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint-Ouen-sur-Seine), burning a 50/50-mix of coal and [wood pellets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wood_pellets).[^cite_note-316]

### Water and sanitation



The Seine, the [Île de la Cité](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%8Ele_de_la_Cit%C3%A9) and a [Bateau Mouche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bateau_Mouche)

Paris in its early history had only the rivers Seine and [Bièvre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bi%C3%A8vre_\(river\)) for water. From 1809, the [Canal de l'Ourcq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canal_de_l'Ourcq) provided Paris with water from less-polluted rivers to the north-east of the capital.[^cite_note-317] From 1857, the civil engineer [Eugène Belgrand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eug%C3%A8ne_Belgrand), under [Napoleon III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleon_III), oversaw the construction of a series of new aqueducts that brought water from locations all around the city to several reservoirs.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurchell197193-318]

From then on, the new reservoir system became Paris's principal source of drinking water. The remains of the old system, pumped into lower levels of the same reservoirs, were from then on used for the cleaning of Paris's streets. This system is still a major part of Paris's water-supply network. Today, Paris has more than 2,400 km (1,491 mi) of underground sewers.[^cite_note-sewers-319]

[Air pollution](10934212.md) in Paris, from the point of view of [particulate matter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Particulates) (PM10), is the highest in France with 38 μg/m<sup>3</sup>.[^cite_note-320] Paris has one of the highest levels of nitrogen dioxide pollution in the EU.[^cite_note-321]

### Parks and gardens

Main articles: [List of parks and gardens in Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_parks_and_gardens_in_Paris) and [History of Parks and Gardens of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Parks_and_Gardens_of_Paris)



The lawns of the [Parc des Buttes-Chaumont](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parc_des_Buttes-Chaumont) on a sunny day

Paris has more than 421 municipal parks and gardens, covering more than 3,000 hectares and containing more than 250,000 trees.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJarrassé20076-322] Two of Paris's oldest and most famous gardens are the [Tuileries Garden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuileries_Garden), created in 1564 for the [Tuileries Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuileries_Palace) and redone by [André Le Nôtre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andr%C3%A9_Le_N%C3%B4tre) between 1664 and 1672,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELawrenceGondrand2010125-323] and the [Luxembourg Garden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luxembourg_Garden), for the [Luxembourg Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luxembourg_Palace), built for [Marie de' Medici](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marie_de'_Medici) in 1612, which today houses the [Senate](567323.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELawrenceGondrand2010208-324] The *[Jardin des plantes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jardin_des_plantes)* was the first botanical garden in Paris, created in 1626.[^cite_note-325]

Between 1853 and 1870, Emperor [Napoleon III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleon_III) and the city's first director of parks and gardens, [Jean-Charles Adolphe Alphand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Charles_Adolphe_Alphand), created the [Bois de Boulogne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bois_de_Boulogne), [Bois de Vincennes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bois_de_Vincennes), [Parc Montsouris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parc_Montsouris) and [Parc des Buttes-Chaumont](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parc_des_Buttes-Chaumont), located at the four compass points around Paris, as well as many smaller parks, squares and gardens in the Paris's quarters.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJarrassé2007122–161-326] Since 1977, the city has created 166 new parks, most notably the [Parc de la Villette](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parc_de_la_Villette) (1987), [Parc André Citroën](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parc_Andr%C3%A9_Citro%C3%ABn) (1992), [Parc de Bercy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parc_de_Bercy) (1997) and [Parc Clichy-Batignolles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parc_Clichy-Batignolles_%E2%80%93_Martin-Luther-King) (2007).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJarrassé2007242–256-327] One of the newest parks, the [Promenade des Berges de la Seine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Promenade_des_Berges_de_la_Seine) (2013), has floating gardens.

### Cemeteries



The [Paris Catacombs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catacombs_of_Paris) hold the remains of approximately 6 million people.

During the Roman era, Paris's main cemetery was located on the outskirts of the [left bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rive_Gauche) settlement. This changed with the rise of Catholic Christianity, where almost every inner-city church had adjoining burial grounds for use by their parishes. With Paris's growth, many of these, particularly the city's largest cemetery, the [Holy Innocents' Cemetery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Innocents'_Cemetery), were filled to overflowing. When inner-city burials were condemned from 1786, the contents of all Paris's parish cemeteries were transferred to a renovated section of [Paris's stone mines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mines_of_Paris), today [place Denfert-Rochereau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Place_Denfert-Rochereau) in the 14th arrondissement.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWhaley2012101-328][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBroadwell200792-329]

After a tentative creation of several smaller suburban cemeteries, the Prefect Nicholas Frochot under [Napoleon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleon) Bonaparte provided a more definitive solution in the creation of three massive Parisian cemeteries outside the city limits.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAyers2004271-330] Open from 1804, these were the cemeteries of [Père Lachaise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P%C3%A8re_Lachaise_Cemetery), [Montmartre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montmartre_Cemetery), [Montparnasse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montparnasse_Cemetery), and later [Passy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Passy_Cemetery). New suburban cemeteries were created in the early 20th century: The largest of these are the [Cimetière parisien de Saint-Ouen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint-Ouen_Cemetery), the [Cimetière parisien de Pantin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cimeti%C3%A8re_parisien_de_Pantin), also known as Cimetière parisien de [Pantin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pantin)-[Bobigny](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bobigny), the Cimetière parisien d'[Ivry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivry-sur-Seine), and the Cimetière parisien de [Bagneux](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bagneux,_Hauts-de-Seine).[^cite_note-Parisian_Cemeteries-331] Famous people buried in Parisian cemeteries include [Oscar Wilde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oscar_Wilde), [Frédéric Chopin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fr%C3%A9d%C3%A9ric_Chopin), [Jim Morrison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Morrison), [Édith Piaf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89dith_Piaf) and [Serge Gainsbourg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serge_Gainsbourg).[^cite_note-332]

### Education

Main article: [Education in Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Paris)



The [Sorbonne University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sorbonne_University)



[HEC Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HEC_Paris)

In 2009, around 40 percent of Parisians held a *[licence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Licence_\(France\))*-level diploma or higher, the highest proportion in France.[^cite_note-333] 13 percent have no diploma, the third-lowest percentage in France. Education in Paris and the Île-de-France region employs approximately 330,000 people, 170,000 of whom are teachers and professors, teaching approximately 2.9 million students in around 9,000 primary, secondary, and higher education schools and institutions.[^cite_note-idf_education-334]

The [University of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Paris), founded in the 12th century, is often called the [Sorbonne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Paris) after one of its original medieval colleges. In 1970, it was broken up into [thirteen autonomous universities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_universities_and_institutions_in_the_Paris_region) following the [student demonstrations in 1968](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/May_68). Most of the campuses today are in the [Latin Quarter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_Quarter,_Paris), where the old university was located, while others are scattered around the city and the suburbs.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECombeau2013213–214-335]

The Paris region hosts France's highest concentration of the *[grandes écoles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grandes_%C3%A9coles)* – 55 specialised centres of higher education outside or inside the public university structure. The prestigious public universities are usually considered *[grands établissements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grands_%C3%A9tablissements)*. Most of the *grandes écoles* were relocated to the suburbs of Paris in the 1960s and 1970s, in new campuses much larger than the old campuses within the crowded City of Paris. The [École Normale Supérieure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89cole_Normale_Sup%C3%A9rieure), [PSL University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_Sciences_et_Lettres_University) has remained on rue d'Ulm in the [5th arrondissement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/5th_arrondissement_of_Paris).[^cite_note-336]

Paris is the home of prestigious universities in science and technology ([Conservatoire national des arts et métiers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservatoire_national_des_arts_et_m%C3%A9tiers), [Polytechnic Institute of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polytechnic_Institute_of_Paris), [Paris Cité University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_Cit%C3%A9_University), [Paris-Saclay University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris-Saclay_University), [Sorbonne University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sorbonne_University)), political science ([Sciences Po](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sciences_Po)),[^cite_note-337] management ([HEC Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HEC_Paris),[^cite_note-338] [ESSEC Business School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ESSEC_Business_School), [ESCP Business School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ESCP_Business_School), [INSEAD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/INSEAD))[^cite_note-339] [archaeology](18951655.md), [art history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_history), [anthropology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthropology) and [epigraphy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigraphy) ([École du Louvre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89cole_du_Louvre)), as well as multidisciplinary universities ([Paris Sciences et Lettres University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_Sciences_et_Lettres_University)).[^cite_note-340]

### Healthcare



The [Hôtel-Dieu de Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H%C3%B4tel-Dieu_de_Paris) is the oldest hospital in Paris.

Health care and emergency medical service in the City of Paris and its suburbs are provided by the [Assistance publique – Hôpitaux de Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assistance_publique_%E2%80%93_H%C3%B4pitaux_de_Paris) (AP-HP), a public hospital system that employs more than 90,000 people, including practitioners, support personnel, and administrators, in 44 hospitals.[^cite_note-hospital-341] It is the largest hospital system in Europe. The hospitals receive more than 5.8 million annual patient visits.[^cite_note-hospital-341]

One of the most notable hospitals is the [Hôtel-Dieu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H%C3%B4tel-Dieu_de_Paris), founded in 651, the oldest hospital in Paris and the oldest worldwide still operating,[^cite_note-342] although the current building is the product of a reconstruction of 1877. Other hospitals include [Pitié-Salpêtrière Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Piti%C3%A9-Salp%C3%AAtri%C3%A8re_Hospital), one of the largest in Europe, [Hôpital Cochin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H%C3%B4pital_Cochin), [Bichat–Claude Bernard Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bichat%E2%80%93Claude_Bernard_Hospital), [Hôpital Européen Georges-Pompidou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H%C3%B4pital_Europ%C3%A9en_Georges-Pompidou), [Bicêtre Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bic%C3%AAtre_Hospital), [Beaujon Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beaujon_Hospital), the [Curie Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Curie_Institute_\(Paris\)), [Lariboisière Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lariboisi%C3%A8re_Hospital), [Necker–Enfants Malades Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Necker%E2%80%93Enfants_Malades_Hospital), [Hôpital Saint-Louis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H%C3%B4pital_Saint-Louis), [Hôpital de la Charité](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H%C3%B4pital_de_la_Charit%C3%A9) and the [American Hospital of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Hospital_of_Paris).

## International relations

### International organisations

The [United Nations Educational, Scientific and Cultural Organization](21786641.md) (UNESCO) has had its headquarters in Paris since November 1958. Paris is also the home of the [Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organisation_for_Economic_Co-operation_and_Development) (OECD).[^cite_note-343] Paris hosts the headquarters of the [European Space Agency](10363.md), the [International Energy Agency](285292.md), [European Securities and Markets Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Securities_and_Markets_Authority) and the [European Banking Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Banking_Authority).

### Twin towns – sister cities

See also: [List of twin towns and sister cities in France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_twin_towns_and_sister_cities_in_France)

Since April 1956, Paris is exclusively and reciprocally [twinned](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sister_city) with [Rome](25458.md), Italy.[^cite_note-IR-344][^cite_note-345]

> *Seule Paris est digne de Rome; seule Rome est digne de Paris.* (in French)\
> &#x20;*Solo Parigi è degna di Roma; solo Roma è degna di Parigi.* (in Italian)\
> &#x20;"Only Paris is worthy of Rome; only Rome is worthy of Paris."[^cite_note-346][^cite_note-347][^cite_note-Paris1-348][^cite_note-Paris2-349][^cite_note-350]

## In popular culture

Paris has been the setting for many movies, TV shows, and other forms of media, including the Netflix streaming series [Emily in Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emily_in_Paris) and the kids' animated superhero show [Miraculous Ladybug](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miraculous_Ladybug).
